A.7 Further Connections A.7.1 Connection Between the Noise Geometry and the Energy Score Loss A.7.2 Denoising A.8 Model Architectures and Training License: arXiv.org perpetual non-exclusive license arXiv:2512.23818v3 [stat.ML] 07 Nov 2026 Energy-Tweedie: Score meets Score, Energy meets Energy Andrej Leban Affiliation: Department of Statistics, Affiliation: University of Michigan, Affiliation: Ann Arbor, MI, United States Email: leban@umich.edu Abstract Denoising and score estimation are classically linked through Tweedie’s
formula, which relates the posterior mean under Gaussian noise to the Stein score of the noisy marginal.
In this work, we extend this perspective beyond Gaussian noise to a broad class of Gibbs (energy-based) noise distributions, with the generalized Gaussian family as an example.
We derive the Energy-Tweedie identity: when the denoising posterior is
viewed through the lens of scoring rules, the path derivative of a kernel scoring rule defined by the noise potential recovers the Stein score
of the noisy marginal.
Thus, the familiar correspondence between Gaussian noise, posterior means, squared loss, and Tweedie’s formula is lifted to a distributional correspondence between Gibbs noise distributions, full posterior laws, kernel scoring rules, and the
Energy-Tweedie identity, which gives a Tweedie-style relation for each noise potential.
Among its consequences, this identity gives a posterior-samples-to-score route to score estimation, yields a principled criterion for
estimating unknown noise parameters, and enables diffusion-style sampling along user-chosen paths through the noise-parameter space, supplying the score-based perspective on recent generative methods trained with scoring rules. 1 1 1 Code repository: https://github.com/andleb/et . 1 Introduction Denoising, empirical Bayes, and score-based generative modeling are classically tied together by Tweedie’s formula. Under additive Gaussian noise Y = X + ϵ Y=X+\epsilon , the posterior mean 𝔼 ⁡ [ X ∣ Y = y ] {\mathbb{E}}[X\mid Y=y] is the MSE-optimal denoiser and its residual determines the Stein score s m ​ ( y ) = ∇ y ​ log ​ m ​ ( y ) s_{m}(y)=\nabla_{y}\log m(y) of the noisy marginal.
This correspondence is one reason Gaussian denoising occupies such a central position in score-based diffusion.
Once the noise is non-Gaussian, however, the posterior mean is typically no longer the natural object exposed by the score. Our main result, the Energy-Tweedie identity, connects the score of the noisy marginal to the path derivative of a scoring rule determined by the noise potential (such as the energy score ), and evaluated at the denoising posterior. 2 2 2 Somewhat coincidentally, we thus connect two pairs of previously-unrelated homonyms: both energy and score have double meanings. The familiar correspondence between Gaussian noise, posterior means, squared loss, and Tweedie’s formula is thus lifted to
a correspondence between Gibbs noise, full posterior laws, kernel scoring rules, and the Energy-Tweedie identity,
as established in Theorem 20 . Cor. 4.3 provides a summary of the correspondence using the contrast between the Gaussian and generalized Gaussian cases.
The main contributions of this work are thus: • A general identity. The Energy-Tweedie identity provides the noisy-marginal score for any normalizable Gibbs noise distribution (Thm. 20 ), establishing a connection between the former and scoring rules. • A posterior-samples-to-score route. Any sufficiently accurate sampler of the denoising posterior becomes a score estimator. • Noise-parameter estimation. We propose an M-estimation method recovering
all the unknown noise parameters of any Gibbs noise distribution from noisy data, with theoretical guarantees for the generalized Gaussian case. • Diffusion along noise-parameter paths. We show how posterior models can be used inside traditional score-based samplers beyond Gaussian noising, with the path through the noise parameters a design variable chosen freely at sampling time. 2 Background 2.1 Denoising, Tweedie’s Formula, and Score Matching We denote the clean data as X ∼ p X\sim p and the noise, independent of X X , as ϵ ∼ q \epsilon\sim q , where q q comes from a location-equivariant family.
Let the noisy data be Y = X + ϵ Y=X+\epsilon , with X , ϵ , Y ∈ ℝ d X,\epsilon,Y\in\mathbb{R}^{d} . The noisy marginal is then: m ⁡ ( y ) = ( p ∗ q ) ​ ( y ) = ∫ p ⁡ ( x ) ​ q ​ ( y − x ) ​ 𝑑 x , m(y)\;=\;(p*q)(y)\;=\;\int p(x)\,q(y-x)\,dx, (1) where “ ∗ * ” denotes the convolution operation. The Stein score of the noisy marginal is then defined as: s m ​ ( y ) = ∇ y ​ log ​ m ​ ( y ) . s_{m}(y)\;=\;\nabla_{y}\log m(y). (2) A well-known result for s m s_{m} is Fisher’s formula [ 21 ; 5 ] . In our case, we
will be plugging in y y — the “noisy” ambient variable — instead of the parameter θ \theta ; that is, switching
from the Fisher to the Stein score. The result when using additive noise q q is: s m ​ ( y ) = ∫ 𝒳 ∇ y ′ ​ log ​ q ​ ( y ′ − x ) | y ′ = y ​ p ​ ( x ∣ Y = y ) ​ 𝑑 x , s_{m}(y)=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\nabla_{y^{\prime}}\log q(y^{\prime}-x)|_{y^{\prime}=y}~p(x\mid Y=y)\,dx, (3) or equivalently: s m ( y ) = 𝔼 X ∼ P ( ⋅ | Y = y ) [ ∇ y log q ( y − X ) ] . s_{m}(y)={\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim P(\cdot|Y=y)}\left[\nabla_{y}\log q(y-X)\right]. (4) Eq. 4 can be seen as a “generalized Tweedie” formula. Setting q = 𝒩 ⁡ ( 0 , Σ ) q={\mathcal{N}}(0,\Sigma) , we
arrive at the “standard” Tweedie’s formula [ 29 ; 10 ] : 𝔼 ⁡ [ X ∣ Y = y ] = y + Σ ​ s m ​ ( y ) {\mathbb{E}}[X\mid Y=y]\,=\,y+\Sigma\,s_{m}(y) (5) The derivation and more details can be found in App. A.1.1 . 2.2 Scoring Rules and the Energy Score Scoring rules were introduced as a measure for the evaluation of probabilistic forecasts: thus, they take
in the predictive distribution and the value (taken to be from the ground truth distribution) that materializes,
outputting a single value — the score [ 12 ; 27 ; 11 ] .
For a general kernel ρ ⁡ ( x , y ) \rho(x,y) , the kernel score is defined as: S ρ ​ ( P , y ) = 𝔼 Y ∼ P ​ [ ρ ⁡ ( Y , y ) ] − 1 2 ​ 𝔼 Y , Y ′ ∼ iid P ​ [ ρ ⁡ ( Y , Y ′ ) ] , S_{\rho}(P,y)=\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim P}[\rho(Y,y)]-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{Y,Y^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}P}[\rho(Y,Y^{\prime})], (6) where P P is the predictive distribution, and y y is a sample from the true distribution.

We have adopted a negative sign before the scoring rule in order to match the usual objective minimization convention in
machine learning.
The first term measures the distance between the forecast and the observations and would be minimized by a collapsed point forecast. The second term rewards admitting uncertainty; together, the two terms make honest reporting optimal in expectation.
In this convention, a scoring rule is said to be proper if 𝔼 y ∼ Q ​ [ S ρ ​ ( P , y ) ] ≥ 𝔼 y ∼ Q ​ [ S ρ ​ ( Q , y ) ] . {\mathbb{E}}_{y\sim Q}[S_{\rho}(P,y)]\geq{\mathbb{E}}_{y\sim Q}[S_{\rho}(Q,y)]. It is said to be strictly proper if we have the equality above iff P ≡ Q P\equiv Q — the expected
scoring rule is minimized uniquely by the ground truth distribution.
A common example is the energy score , introduced in Gneiting and Raftery [12] , ES β ​ ( P , y ) = 𝔼 Y ∼ P ​ [ ‖ Y − y ‖ 2 β ] − 1 2 ​ 𝔼 Y , Y ′ ​ ∼ iid ​ P ​ [ ‖ Y − Y ′ ‖ 2 β ] , \mathrm{ES}_{\beta}(P,y)={\mathbb{E}}_{Y\sim P}\left[\|Y-y\|_{2}^{\beta}\right]-\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\mathbb{E}}_{Y,Y^{\prime}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}P}\left[\|Y-Y^{\prime}\|_{2}^{\beta}\right], (7) where β > 0 \beta>0 , and ρ ⁡ ( x , y ) = ‖ x − y ‖ 2 β \rho(x,y)=\|x-y\|_{2}^{\beta} is the kernel in this case.
While existing work has covered ∥ ⋅ ∥ α \|\cdot\|_{\alpha} with α \alpha a scalar,

we will be focusing on the Mahalanobis distance (for some Σ ≻ 0 \Sigma\succ 0 ): ρ Σ − 1 ​ ( x , y ) = ‖ x − y ‖ Σ − 1 = ( x − y ) ⊤ ​ Σ − 1 ​ ( x − y ) \rho_{\Sigma^{-1}}(x,y)=\|x-y\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}=\sqrt{(x-y)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}(x-y)} (8) and the β \beta -th power of the corresponding norm ∥ ⋅ ∥ Σ − 1 \|\cdot\|_{\Sigma^{-1}} in our examples.

The corresponding (negative) scoring rule is: ES Σ − 1 , β ​ ( P , y ) = 𝔼 Y ∼ P ​ [ ‖ Y − y ‖ Σ − 1 β ] − 1 2 ​ 𝔼 Y , Y ′ ​ ∼ iid ​ P ​ [ ‖ Y − Y ′ ‖ Σ − 1 β ] \mathrm{ES}_{\Sigma^{-1},\beta}(P,y)~=~{\mathbb{E}}_{Y\sim P}\left[\|Y-y\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta}\right]-\tfrac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}}_{Y,Y^{\prime}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}P}\left[\left\|Y-Y^{\prime}\right\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta}\right] (9) 3 Tweedie-Like Formula for Gibbs Noise We start this section by further specifying the family of q q we will be considering from now on: Definition 3.1 (Noise potential and the Gibbs distribution) Let ℰ : ℝ d → [ 0 , ∞ ) {\mathcal{E}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty) be a measurable and symmetric noise potential with ℰ ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 {\mathcal{E}}(0)=0 .

The matched Gibbs distribution is defined as: q ℰ , c ​ ( u ) = 1 Z ℰ ​ ( c ) ​ e − c ​ ℰ ​ ( u ) , q_{{\mathcal{E}},c}(u)=\frac{1}{Z_{\mathcal{E}}(c)}e^{-c\,{\mathcal{E}}(u)}, (10) where c c is the scale parameter and Z ℰ ​ ( c ) = ∫ ℝ d e − c ​ ℰ ​ ( u ) ​ 𝑑 u Z_{\mathcal{E}}(c)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-c\,{\mathcal{E}}(u)}\,du (11) is the normalizing constant, which we require to be finite. Thus, the admissible scales form the set: Λ ℰ = { c > 0 : Z ℰ ​ ( c ) < ∞ } . \Lambda_{\mathcal{E}}=\bigl\{c>0:\ Z_{\mathcal{E}}(c)<\infty\bigr\}. (12) That is, we (only) require normalizability. The density is thus part of the broader location-equivariant family.
Note that the potential ℰ {\mathcal{E}} itself can include further parameters, hence the distribution is parameterized by ( ℰ , c ) ({\mathcal{E}},c) ,
where the former is to be understood as a particular choice of the functional form.
In machine learning, this distributional form is a version of what is commonly known as an energy model [ 24 ] .
We get for the score of the noising kernel: ∇ y ​ log ​ q ℰ , c ​ ( y − X ) = − c ​ ∇ y ℰ ​ ( y − X ) \nabla_{y}\log q_{{\mathcal{E}},c}(y-X)=-c\,\nabla_{y}{\mathcal{E}}(y-X) (13) We will be plugging this into Eq. 4 , and adopting the “path derivative” notation from
Variational Inference literature [ 30 ] : let ∇ PD \nabla^{\mathrm{PD}} denote a “fixed-measure”
gradient — taking the gradient of the function, but not the measure, exactly as the operation is performed in
Eq. 3 : ∇ y PD 𝔼 X ∼ P ( ⋅ ∣ Y = y ) [ f ( y , X ) ] = Δ ∫ 𝒳 ∇ y ′ f ( y ′ , x ) | y ′ = y p ( x ∣ Y = y ) d x . \nabla^{\mathrm{PD}}_{y}\,{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim P(\cdot\mid Y=y)}[f(y,X)]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}~\int_{\mathcal{X}}\nabla_{y^{\prime}}f(y^{\prime},x)|_{y^{\prime}=y}~p(x\mid Y=y)\,dx. (14) Another terminology (stemming from the implementation) is the stop-gradient ( sg ) operation applied
to the distribution; namely: ∇ y PD 𝔼 X ∼ P ( ⋅ ∣ Y = y ) [ f ( y , X ) ] = 𝔼 X ∼ P ( ⋅ ∣ Y = y ) [ ∇ y f ( y , sg ( X ) ) ] . \nabla_{y}^{\mathrm{PD}}{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim P(\cdot\mid Y=y)}[f(y,X)]={\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim P(\cdot\mid Y=y)}\left[\nabla_{y}f(y,\operatorname{sg}(X))\right]. We then arrive at the following Tweedie-like formula for Gibbs noise: Proposition 3.2 (Tweedie-Like Formula for Gibbs Noise) For Gibbs noise distributions under the following assumptions: • (A1) c ∈ Λ ℰ c\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{E}} ; • (A2) the generalized Tweedie formula (Eq. 4 ) holds at y y for q ℰ , c q_{{\mathcal{E}},c} ; • (A3) ℰ {\mathcal{E}} is differentiable outside a Lebesgue-null set N N with P ⁡ ( ( y − X ) ∈ N ∣ Y = y ) = 0 P((y-X)\in N\mid Y=y)=0 ; • (A4) 𝔼 ⁡ [ ‖ ∇ ℰ ​ ( y − X ) ‖ ∣ Y = y ] < ∞ {\mathbb{E}}\bigl[\|\nabla{\mathcal{E}}(y-X)\|\mid Y=y\bigr]<\infty . Eq. 4 becomes: s m ​ ( y ) = 𝔼 X ​ [ − c ​ ∇ y ℰ ​ ( y − X ) ∣ Y = y ] = − c ​ ∇ y PD 𝔼 X ​ [ ℰ ⁡ ( y − X ) ∣ Y = y ] \boxed{s_{m}(y)={\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[-c\,\nabla_{y}{\mathcal{E}}(y-X)\mid Y=y\right]=-c\,\nabla_{y}^{\mathrm{PD}}~{\mathbb{E}}_{X}\left[{\mathcal{E}}(y-X)\mid Y=y\right]} (15) A short proof can be found in App. A.2.1 .

In words, the score at y y coincides with the negative
path-gradient (w.r.t. y y ) of the expected (w.r.t. the posterior) potential centered around the same y y . In the case of the exponential family of distributions, Efron [10] has derived related Tweedie’s formulas for the first two moments.
We would like to note that a Gibbs distribution is not necessarily an exponential family
distribution when viewed as a family in the location parameter x x ; this result thus provides Tweedie identities beyond [ 10 ] . Some examples follow below. 3.1 Examples Three familiar special cases illustrate which summary of the denoising posterior the score encodes;
derivations are deferred to App. A.2.2 . For Gaussian noise, ℰ ⁡ ( u ) = ‖ u ‖ Σ − 1 2 {\mathcal{E}}(u)=\|u\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2} , with c = 1 2 c=\frac{1}{2} constant, and Eq. 15 recovers the ordinary Tweedie
residual: s m ​ ( y ) = Σ − 1 ​ ( 𝔼 ⁡ [ X ∣ Y = y ] − y ) . s_{m}(y)=\Sigma^{-1}\left({\mathbb{E}}[X\mid Y=y]-y\right). The score therefore points toward the posterior mean in Σ − 1 \Sigma^{-1} geometry. For multivariate Laplace noise, q ⁡ ( u ) ∝ exp ⁡ ( − λ ​ ‖ u ‖ Σ − 1 ) q(u)\propto\exp(-\lambda\|u\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}) , hence ℰ ⁡ ( u ) = ‖ u ‖ Σ − 1 {\mathcal{E}}(u)=\|u\|_{\Sigma^{-1}} and c = λ c=\lambda , and
the score averages only directions from y y to posterior samples: s m ​ ( y ) = − λ ​ 𝔼 ​ [ Σ − 1 ​ ( y − X ) ‖ y − X ‖ Σ − 1 | Y = y ] . s_{m}(y)=-\lambda\,{\mathbb{E}}\!\left[\frac{\Sigma^{-1}(y-X)}{\|y-X\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}}\,\middle|\,Y=y\right]. This replaces the Gaussian’s mean-seeking behavior with a more robust, approximately median-seeking behavior, pointing toward the posterior geometric median in the same metric. Generalized Gaussian We use the parameterization: q ⁡ ( u ) ∝ exp ⁡ ( − λ β ​ ‖ u ‖ Σ − 1 β ) , q(u)\ \propto\ \exp\Big(-\frac{\lambda}{\beta}~\|u\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta}\Big), (16) for β , λ > 0 \beta,\lambda>0 , which relates to the common scale parameter α \alpha via α = ( β / λ ) 1 / β \alpha=({\beta}/{\lambda})^{1/\beta} [ 48 ] .
Thus ℰ ⁡ ( u ) = ‖ u ‖ Σ − 1 β {\mathcal{E}}(u)=\|u\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta} and c = λ β c=\frac{\lambda}{\beta} .
The normalizing constant is finite for all c > 0 c>0 , so Λ ℰ = ( 0 , ∞ ) \Lambda_{\mathcal{E}}=(0,\infty) .

This is a location family, but not generally an exponential family distribution in the denoising setting with unknown location parameter x x ;
the Gaussian case is recovered at ( β = 2 , λ = 1 ) (\beta=2,\lambda=1) . Lemma 3.3 Inheriting the assumptions of Prop. 3.2 , generalized Gaussian noise has score s m ​ ( y ) = − λ ​ Σ − 1 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ ‖ y − X ‖ Σ − 1 β − 2 ​ ( y − X ) ∣ Y = y ] . s_{m}(y)=-\lambda~\Sigma^{-1}\,{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|y-X\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta-2}~(y-X)\mid Y=y\right]. (17) A short proof and the verification of the assumptions can be found in App. A.2.3 .
The factor ‖ y − X ‖ Σ − 1 β − 2 \|y-X\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta-2} controls which posterior samples dominate: 1 < β < 2 1<\beta<2 values trade off local (Laplace-like) robustness against distance-sensitive (Gaussian-like) residual weighting. This generalized Gaussian family will be used in the practical examples. 4 The Energy-Tweedie Identity Define the discrepancy induced by the noise potential ℰ {\mathcal{E}} as: ρ ⁡ ( x , y ) = ℰ ⁡ ( y − x ) . \rho(x,y)={\mathcal{E}}(y-x). (18) As the noise potential is symmetric with ℰ ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 {\mathcal{E}}(0)=0 , we have ρ ⁡ ( x , y ) = ρ ⁡ ( y , x ) \rho(x,y)=\rho(y,x) and ρ ⁡ ( x , x ) = 0 \rho(x,x)=0 .
Next, the path-derivative of the associated kernel score (Eq. 6 ) is: ∇ y PD S ρ ​ ( P , y ) = ∇ y PD ( 𝔼 X ∼ P ​ [ ℰ ⁡ ( y − X ) ] − 1 2 ​ 𝔼 X , X ′ ​ ∼ iid ​ P ​ [ ℰ ⁡ ( X ′ − X ) ] ) = 𝔼 X ∼ P ​ [ ∇ y ℰ ​ ( y − X ) ] . \nabla^{\mathrm{PD}}_{y}S_{\rho}(P,y)~=~\nabla^{\mathrm{PD}}_{y}\left({\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim P}[{\mathcal{E}}(y-X)]-\tfrac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}}_{X,X^{\prime}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}P}[{\mathcal{E}}(X^{\prime}-X)]\right)~=~{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim P}[\nabla_{y}{\mathcal{E}}(y-X)]. (19) We are interested in what this scoring rule tells us in the denoising context: i.e., adopt P P to be the denoising
posterior P = P ⁡ ( X ∣ Y = y ) P=P(X\mid Y=y) and examine the path-gradient above when the latter is supplied with a noisy sample y y .
We obtain after comparison with Prop. 3.2 : Theorem 4.1 (The Energy-Tweedie Identity) For data X ∼ p X\sim p noised with Gibbs noise (Eq. 10 ) with the noise potential ℰ {\mathcal{E}} ,
the associated discrepancy ρ \rho and the scale parameter c c , assume at a noisy data sample y y a finite ρ \rho -moment: 𝔼 X , X ′ ∼ iid P ⁡ ( X ∣ Y = y ) ​ [ ρ ⁡ ( X , X ′ ) ] < ∞ . \mathbb{E}_{X,X^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{iid}}}{{\sim}}P(X\mid Y=y)}\left[\rho(X,X^{\prime})\right]<\infty. Under the assumptions of Prop. 3.2 , the Energy-Tweedie identity then links the Stein score of
the noisy marginal to the (path-derivative) gradient of the induced kernel score at the denoising posterior: s m ​ ( y ) = − c ​ ∇ y PD S ρ ​ ( P ⁡ ( X ∣ Y = y ) , y ) . \boxed{s_{m}(y)\;=\;-c\,\nabla_{y}^{\mathrm{PD}}\,S_{\rho}(P(X\mid Y=y),\,y).} (20) In other words, the Stein score of the noisy marginal corresponds to the path-gradient of a matched kernel
score induced by the noise potential with respect to the true posterior. We thus obtain a family of Tweedie-style relations, determined by the noise potential ℰ {\mathcal{E}} . On propriety: the Energy-Tweedie identity (Eq. 20 ) is algebraic and does not depend on the
propriety of the matched scoring rule S ρ S_{\rho} . If one were, however, to plug in an estimator P θ ​ ( X ∣ Y ) P_{\theta}(X\mid Y) ,
it would be preferable for it to be trained via a strictly proper scoring rule; it being S ρ S_{\rho} is unnecessary , though.
The following proposition shows that propriety is determined by the induced discrepancy ρ \rho and hence the noise potential ℰ {\mathcal{E}} alone. Proposition 4.2 (Propriety of the Matched Kernel Score) Independently of (A1)–(A4) in Prop. 3.2 , for the induced S ρ S_{\rho} in Thm. 20 and distributions with finite ρ \rho -moment 𝔼 Z , Z ′ ​ ∼ iid ​ P ​ [ ρ ⁡ ( Z , Z ′ ) ] < ∞ {\mathbb{E}}_{Z,Z^{\prime}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}P}[\rho(Z,Z^{\prime})]<\infty : 1) S ρ S_{\rho} is proper iff ρ \rho is a conditionally negative definite (CND) kernel: ρ \rho is symmetric, with ∑ i = 1 n ∑ j = 1 n a i ​ a j ​ ρ ​ ( z i , z j ) ≤ 0 \sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{i}a_{j}\,\rho(z_{i},z_{j})\leq 0 for all n n , all z 1 , … , z n ∈ ℝ d z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d} , and all a 1 , … , a n ∈ ℝ a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{R} such that ∑ i a i = 0 \sum_{i}a_{i}=0 ; 2) S ρ S_{\rho} is strictly proper iff ρ \rho has strong negative type: the generalized energy distance D ρ ​ ( P , Q ) = 2 ​ 𝔼 Z ∼ P , W ∼ Q ​ [ ρ ⁡ ( Z , W ) ] − 𝔼 Z , Z ′ ​ ∼ iid ​ P ​ [ ρ ⁡ ( Z , Z ′ ) ] − 𝔼 W , W ′ ​ ∼ iid ​ Q ​ [ ρ ⁡ ( W , W ′ ) ] D_{\rho}(P,Q)=2\,{\mathbb{E}}_{Z\sim P,\,W\sim Q}[\rho(Z,W)]-{\mathbb{E}}_{Z,Z^{\prime}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}P}[\rho(Z,Z^{\prime})]-{\mathbb{E}}_{W,W^{\prime}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}Q}[\rho(W,W^{\prime})] vanishes only at P = Q P=Q . If either holds, the finite ρ \rho -moment condition of Thm. 20 is automatically satisfied at the denoising posterior. The proof is given in App. A.3.1 . Corollary 4.3 (The (generalized) Gaussian case) For the generalized Gaussian : ℰ ⁡ ( u ) = ‖ u ‖ Σ − 1 β {\mathcal{E}}(u)=\|u\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta} and c = λ / β c=\lambda/\beta , the matched kernel score is the Mahalanobis energy score (Eq. 9 ): S ρ = ES Σ − 1 , β . S_{\rho}=\mathrm{ES}_{\Sigma^{-1},\beta}. ES Σ − 1 , β \mathrm{ES}_{\Sigma^{-1},\beta} is proper for β ∈ ( 0 , 2 ] \beta\in(0,2] and strictly proper for β ∈ ( 0 , 2 ) \beta\in(0,2) .
In this case, the Energy-Tweedie identity (Eq. 20 ) becomes: s m ​ ( y ) = − λ β ​ ∇ y PD ES Σ − 1 , β ​ ( P ⁡ ( X ∣ Y = y ) , y ) = − λ ​ Σ − 1 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ ‖ y − X ‖ Σ − 1 β − 2 ​ ( y − X ) ∣ Y = y ] . s_{m}(y)=-\frac{\lambda}{\beta}\,\nabla_{y}^{\mathrm{PD}}\,\mathrm{ES}_{\Sigma^{-1},\beta}(P(X\mid Y=y),\,y)\;=\;-\lambda~\Sigma^{-1}\,{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|y-X\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta-2}~(y-X)\mid Y=y\right]. (21) As is expected, we recover the “classical” relations in the ordinary Gaussian case β = 2 , λ = 1 \beta=2,\lambda=1 : D ρ ​ ( P , Q ) = 2 ​ ‖ 𝔼 Z ∼ P ​ [ Z ] − 𝔼 W ∼ Q ​ [ W ] ‖ Σ − 1 2 D_{\rho}(P,Q)=2\,\|\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim P}[Z]-\mathbb{E}_{W\sim Q}[W]\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2} vanishes as soon as the means agree.
As the rule is no longer strictly proper, it pins down only the posterior mean.

We summarize the correspondence below: Object Generalized Gaussian Noise distribution ∝ exp ⁡ ( − λ β ​ ‖ u ‖ Σ − 1 β ) \propto\exp(-\frac{\lambda}{\beta}\|u\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta}) ∝ exp ⁡ ( − 1 2 ​ u ⊤ ​ Σ − 1 ​ u ) \propto\exp(-\frac{1}{2}u^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}u) Objective ES Σ − 1 , β ​ ( P ⁡ ( X ∣ Y = y ) , x ) \mathrm{ES}_{\Sigma^{-1},\beta}(P(X\mid Y=y),x) ‖ 𝔼 ⁡ [ X ∣ Y = y ] − x ‖ Σ − 1 2 \|{\mathbb{E}}[X\mid Y=y]-x\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2} Posterior object P ⁡ ( X ∣ Y = y ) P(X\mid Y=y) 𝔼 ⁡ [ X ∣ Y = y ] {\mathbb{E}}[X\mid Y=y] Score identity − λ β ​ ∇ y PD ES Σ − 1 , β ​ ( P ⁡ ( X ∣ Y = y ) , y ) -\frac{\lambda}{\beta}\nabla_{y}^{\mathrm{PD}}\mathrm{ES}_{\Sigma^{-1},\beta}(P(X\mid Y=y),y) Σ − 1 ​ ( 𝔼 ⁡ [ X ∣ Y = y ] − y ) \Sigma^{-1}({\mathbb{E}}[X\mid Y=y]-y) Proofs of the propriety of ES Σ − 1 , β \mathrm{ES}_{\Sigma^{-1},\beta} and the β = 2 \beta=2 collapse can be found in App. A.3.2 .

For β = 1 \beta=1 and Σ = I d \Sigma=I_{d} we obtain a correspondence between isotropic Laplace noise and the ordinary energy score : s m ​ ( y ) = − λ ​ ∇ y PD ES ​ ( P ⁡ ( X ∣ Y = y ) , y ) s_{m}(y)=-\lambda\nabla^{\mathrm{PD}}_{y}\mathrm{ES}\big(P(X\mid Y=y),y\big) .
In the Gaussian case β = 2 , λ = 1 \beta=2,\lambda=1 , plugging in an estimator trained with a merely proper ( β = 2 \beta=2 ) energy score
suffices; one example is the denoising diffusion model trained via score matching.
Thm. 20 also has a geometric reading, which we develop in App. A.7 for the generalized Gaussian case: in the Riemannian geometry induced by Σ − 1 \Sigma^{-1} , the noisy score is related to the path-derivative of the posterior mean β \beta -energy. Corollary 4.4 (The Student-t case) Take ℰ ⁡ ( u ) = log ⁡ ( 1 + ‖ u ‖ Σ − 1 2 ) {\mathcal{E}}(u)=\log\bigl(1+\|u\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2}\bigr) with c ∈ Λ ℰ = ( d / 2 , ∞ ) c\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{E}}=(d/2,\infty) .
Then q ℰ , c q_{{\mathcal{E}},c} can be identified with the multivariate Student-t distribution by choosing the degrees of freedom ν = 2 ​ c − d \nu=2c-d and the scale matrix Σ / ν \Sigma/\nu , and the identity (Eq. 20 ) holds for every data distribution p p : s m ( y ) = − c ∇ y PD S ρ ( P ( X ∣ Y = y ) , y ) = − 2 c Σ − 1 𝔼 X ∼ P ( ⋅ ∣ Y = y ) [ y − X 1 + ‖ y − X ‖ Σ − 1 2 ] . s_{m}(y)\;=\;-\,c\,\nabla_{y}^{\mathrm{PD}}\,S_{\rho}\bigl(P(X\mid Y=y),\,y\bigr)\;=\;-\,2c\,\Sigma^{-1}\,{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim P(\cdot\mid Y=y)}\!\left[\frac{y-X}{1+\|y-X\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2}}\right]. (22) The induced S ρ S_{\rho} is strictly proper relative to the class of probability measures P P on ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} with 𝔼 Z ∼ P ​ [ log ⁡ ( 1 + ‖ Z ‖ Σ − 1 2 ) ] < ∞ {\mathbb{E}}_{Z\sim P}[\log(1+\|Z\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2})]<\infty . Proofs can be found in App. A.3.2 .

The Student-t case is an example where the theorem gives the score for a heavy-tailed noise distribution even if its mean is undefined ,
with a strictly proper scoring rule. 5 Applications and Empirical Consequences In this section, we will focus on the generalized Gaussian noise case (Cor. 4.3 ) for the concrete examples, as it is a flexible family that includes many of the most common
noise distributions, and the matched kernel score includes the energy score, perhaps the most commonly used multivariate scoring rule. 5.1 Score Estimation Using Theorem 20 one can get a noisy score estimate at any noising distribution
parameter by plugging in a well-specified trained posterior estimator P θ ( ⋅ ∣ Y ) P_{\theta}(\cdot\mid Y) . Using samples from a posterior model X ( i ) ∼ P θ ​ ( X ∣ Y = y ) X^{(i)}\sim P_{\theta}(X\mid Y=y) , the noisy
score Monte Carlo approximation in the generalized Gaussian case (Cor. 4.3 , with the conditional score from Lemma 17 ) is: s ^ m ( y ) = − λ Σ − 1 1 N ∑ i = 1 N ( ∥ y − X ( i ) ∥ Σ − 1 β − 2 ( y − X ( i ) ) ) \widehat{s}_{m}(y)=-\lambda~\Sigma^{-1}~\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\|y-X^{(i)}\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta-2}~(y-X^{(i)})\right) (23) The bias-variance decomposition for this estimator is given in App. A.4.1 .
We wish to examine the clean score estimation aspect.
Write Σ = ε 2 ​ Σ 0 \Sigma=\varepsilon^{2}\Sigma_{0} , and let q ε q_{\varepsilon} be the corresponding generalized Gaussian noising
density. Under suitable regularity conditions, made explicit in App. A.4.3 , the noisy score
converges to the clean data score as ε ↓ 0 \varepsilon\downarrow 0 : s m ε ​ ( y ) = ∇ y ​ log ​ ( p ∗ q ε ) ​ ( y ) ⟶ ∇ y ​ log ​ p ​ ( y ) . s_{m_{\varepsilon}}(y)=\nabla_{y}\log(p*q_{\varepsilon})(y)\longrightarrow\nabla_{y}\log p(y). (24) This approach can be viewed as a distinct route to score estimation: score estimation from the posterior, joining direct score estimation in the line of Hyvärinen [15] and denoising score estimation in the line of Vincent [45] . While adjacent to the latter, it applies to any posterior model P θ ​ ( X ∣ Y ) P_{\theta}(X\mid Y) and does not prescribe the particular training objective.
The Energy-Tweedie identity also allows denoising score estimation, which we denote Energy-Tweedie Denoising Score Matching (ET-DSM). Using Theorem 20 , we learn a neural score field s θ ​ ( y , η ) s_{\theta}(y,\eta) from clean–noisy pairs ( X , Y ) (X,Y) by minimizing ℒ ET ​ - ​ DSM ( θ ; η ) = 𝔼 X ∼ p , Y ∼ q η ( ⋅ ∣ X ) [ ‖ s θ ( Y , η ) + c ∇ Y PD S ρ ( δ X , Y ) ‖ 2 2 ] . \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{ET\text{-}DSM}}(\theta;\eta)={\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim p,\,Y\sim q_{\eta}(\cdot\mid X)}\left[\left\|s_{\theta}(Y,\eta)+c\,\nabla_{Y}^{\mathrm{PD}}S_{\rho}(\delta_{X},Y)\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]. (25) Here δ X \delta_{X} is the point mass at X X , and η \eta denotes the noise parameters for the chosen noise distribution.
For generalized Gaussian noise, the regression target is
given by Eq. 23 with N = 1 N=1 and X ( 1 ) = X X^{(1)}=X .

In practice, the clean score limit (Eq. 24 ) can be approximated by either (or both ) (i) letting the estimator P θ , ε P_{\theta,\varepsilon} take ε \varepsilon as a parameter, and (ii) using numeric techniques such as Richardson
extrapolation [ 28 ] . 3 3 3 Note: this works because q q is in the symmetric location family. Namely, under sufficient regularity conditions, we can define: s ^ RE ​ ( y ) = Δ ε 2 2 ​ s ^ ε 1 ​ ( y ) − ε 1 2 ​ s ^ ε 2 ​ ( y ) ε 2 2 − ε 1 2 \hat{s}_{\mathrm{RE}}(y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\frac{\varepsilon_{2}^{2}\hat{s}_{\varepsilon_{1}}(y)-\varepsilon_{1}^{2}\hat{s}_{\varepsilon_{2}}(y)}{\varepsilon_{2}^{2}-\varepsilon_{1}^{2}} (26) Then: s ^ RE ​ ( y ) = ∇ log ⁡ p ​ ( y ) + 𝒪 ⁡ ( ε 4 ) + ( estimation error ) . \hat{s}_{\mathrm{RE}}(y)=\nabla\log p(y)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4})+(\text{estimation error}). Further details and the derivation for the error order can be found in App. A.4.3 .
We examine this on the synthetic two-dimensional Eight-Gaussians ring distribution commonly used as an example benchmark in generative modeling [ 16 ] .

Throughout the experiments presented in this section,
the generalized Gaussian noise distribution is parameterized by the five-parameter tuple η = ( β , λ , σ , u , v ) \eta=(\beta,\lambda,\sigma,u,v) where Σ = σ 2 ​ S ​ ( u , v ) \Sigma=\sigma^{2}S(u,v) , and σ \sigma sets the noise magnitude. The noise shape ranges over symmetric positive-definite (SPD) matrices of the trace-normalized form S ⁡ ( u , v ) = ( 1 + u v v 1 − u ) . S(u,v)=\begin{pmatrix}1+u&v\\

Details, full comparisons and further results are presented in App. A.4.2 . 5.2 Noise Parameter Estimation Equation 20 also allows us to use the mismatch between the two sides as a loss to learn unknown parameters of the noising distribution: 𝔼 y ∼ m ​ [ ‖ s ^ m ​ ( y ) + c ​ ∇ y PD S ρ ​ ( P ⁡ ( X ∣ Y = y ) , y ) ‖ 2 2 ] , {\mathbb{E}}_{y\sim m}\Big[\Big\|\,\widehat{s}_{m}(y)\;+c\,\nabla_{y}^{\mathrm{PD}}\,S_{\rho}(P(X\mid Y=y),\,y)\Big\|_{2}^{2}\Big], (27) where s ^ m \widehat{s}_{m} is another score estimator that does not require knowledge of the noise parameters,
for example the original score-matching estimator in Hyvärinen [15] or sliced
score matching in Song et al. [38] .
Specializing again to the generalized Gaussian case, we obtain the objective: ( β ^ , λ ^ , Σ ^ ) ∈ arg ​ min β , λ , Σ 𝔼 y ∼ m [ ∥ s ^ m ( y ) + λ β ∇ y PD ES Σ − 1 , β ( P θ ( ⋅ ∣ Y = y ) , y ) ∥ 2 2 ] . (\widehat{\beta},\widehat{\lambda},\widehat{\Sigma})~\in~\argmin_{\beta,\lambda,\Sigma}\ {\mathbb{E}}_{y\sim m}\Big[\Big\|\,\widehat{s}_{m}(y)\;+\;\frac{\lambda}{\beta}\nabla^{\mathrm{PD}}_{y}\mathrm{ES}_{\Sigma^{-1},\beta}\big(P_{\theta}(\cdot\mid Y=y),y\big)\,\Big\|_{2}^{2}\Big]. (28) In equation 28 , we encounter the well-studied scale invariance issue for elliptical
distributions; specifically for the generalized Gaussian (Eq. 16 ), the following set of parameter choices leads to the same density: { ( β , c β / 2 ​ λ , c ​ Σ ) ∣ c ∈ ℝ + } \{(\beta,c^{\beta/2}\,\lambda,c\,\Sigma)\mid c\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\} After adopting a restriction — in our case trace normalization Tr ⁡ ( Σ ) = d \Tr(\Sigma)=d — this orbit has a unique representative, and ( β ∗ , λ ∗ , Σ ∗ ) (\beta^{*},\lambda^{*},\Sigma^{*}) is the unique trace-normalized optimizer. The formal
identifiability statement is Thm. A.1 in Appendix A.5 .
Restricting the trace to the dimensionality of the space is a very common choice in similar settings [ 6 ] .

The scalar scale λ \lambda admits a closed-form solution. Define: G ( y ; β , Σ ) = − 1 β ∇ y PD ES Σ − 1 , β ( P ( ⋅ ∣ Y = y ) , y ) = Σ − 1 𝔼 X ∼ P ( ⋅ ∣ Y = y ) [ ∥ X − y ∥ Σ − 1 β − 2 ( X − y ) ] . G(y;\beta,\Sigma)=-\tfrac{1}{\beta}\nabla^{\mathrm{PD}}_{y}\mathrm{ES}_{\Sigma^{-1},\beta}\big(P(\cdot\mid Y=y),y\big)=\Sigma^{-1}{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim P(\cdot\mid Y=y)}[\|X-y\|^{\beta-2}_{\Sigma^{-1}}(X-y)]. (29) The unconstrained optimizer for λ \lambda in Eq. 28 at the population level is then given by: λ ∗ ​ ( β , Σ ) = 𝔼 Y ∼ m ​ [ ⟨ s m ​ ( Y ) , G ⁡ ( Y , β , Σ ) ⟩ ] 𝔼 Y ∼ m ​ [ ‖ G ⁡ ( Y , β , Σ ) ‖ 2 2 ] . \lambda^{*}(\beta,\Sigma)=\frac{{\mathbb{E}}_{Y\sim m}\left[\langle s_{m}(Y),G(Y;\beta,\Sigma)\rangle\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{Y\sim m}\left[\|G(Y;\beta,\Sigma)\|^{2}_{2}\right]}. (30) The formal statement and Gaussian simplifications are given in Cor. A.3 .
We also show in Prop. A.2 that this noise-parameter estimator is consistent: under empirical L 2 L_{2} consistency of the auxiliary
score estimator s ^ m \widehat{s}_{m} and the Monte Carlo posterior field, approximate minimizers of the plug-in criterion converge to the normalized true parameter tuple ( β ∗ , λ ∗ , Σ ∗ ) (\beta^{*},\lambda^{*},\Sigma^{*}) .
The implementation is summarized in Alg. 1 in Appendix A.5 : parameterize a trace-normalized Σ \Sigma , estimate G G from posterior samples, profile λ \lambda , and take gradient steps over the remaining parameters.
We test this profiled estimator in the same full five-parameter setting ( β , λ , σ , u , v ) (\beta,\lambda,\sigma,u,v) as in Sec. 5.1 .
One joint fit over three known σ ∈ { 0.25 , 0.45 , 0.75 } \sigma\in\{0.25,0.45,0.75\} levels estimates ( β , λ , u , v ) (\beta,\lambda,u,v) .
Here, we write Σ j = σ j 2 ​ S \Sigma_{j}=\sigma_{j}^{2}S and normalize tr ⁡ ( S ) = d \operatorname{tr}(S)=d as σ j \sigma_{j} are known factors.

and over the fifteen fits (3 trained posterior models × \times 5 replicates) when it is learned. Forcing Gaussianity compensates for the imposed light tails by shifting λ ^ \widehat{\lambda} down, while imposing isotropy drags β ^ \widehat{\beta} down to 1.12 1.12 , imitating the missing anisotropy with heavier tails.
What provides accuracy is thus being able to fit the correct noise family, enabled by our approach, which “survives” plugging in an auxiliary score estimator almost untouched.
Tail weight and anisotropic geometry are common in real noise, yet methods for estimating
unknown noise parameters typically estimate only the scalar noise scale, fixing an isotropic Gaussian or a similar exponential-family model [ 17 ] .
Such methods operate within the isotropic Gaussian case of
Table 2 ; here that is fitted with oracle values against an M-estimation parameter, so no estimator confined to that class is likely to do better.
The complete 16 16 -row comparison in App. A.5 shows that the same family ordering persists across all four source combinations, confirming that the misspecification is due to the noise family restriction. 5.3 Energy-Tweedie Diffusion One can use Thm. 20 to sample from p ⁡ ( x ) p(x) in a diffusion-like approach by using a time-parameterized model P θ ​ ( X ∣ Y , t ) P_{\theta}(X\mid Y,t) .
Specializing again to the generalized Gaussian case, the construction is: Noise-parameter path. Choose t ↦ ( β t , λ t , Σ t ) t\mapsto(\beta_{t},\lambda_{t},\Sigma_{t}) , t ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] t\in[0,1] , and pass t t (or the corresponding noise parameters) as conditioning information. Train. Fit P θ ( ⋅ ∣ Y , t ) P_{\theta}(\cdot\mid Y,t) with a proper scoring rule, for example the matched
non-Euclidean energy score in Eq. 31 . Sample. Draw X ( i ) ∼ P θ ( ⋅ ∣ Y = y k , t k ) X^{(i)}\sim P_{\theta}(\cdot\mid Y=y_{k},t_{k}) and use the Monte Carlo
Energy-Tweedie score in Eq. 32 inside annealed Langevin or another score-based
sampler. The matched training objective is min θ 𝔼 t ∼ Unif ⁡ ( [ 0 , 1 ] ) 𝔼 x ∼ p 𝔼 y | x , t [ ES Σ t − 1 , β t ( P θ ( ⋅ ∣ Y = y , t ) , x ) ] , \min_{\theta}\ {\mathbb{E}}_{t\sim\mathrm{Unif}([0,1])}\,{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim p}\,{\mathbb{E}}_{y\mid x,t}\Big[\,\mathrm{ES}_{\Sigma_{t}^{-1},\beta_{t}}\big(P_{\theta}(\cdot\mid Y=y,t),\,x\big)\Big], (31) where y = x + ϵ t , ϵ t ∼ q β t , λ t , Σ t y=x+\epsilon_{t},\ \epsilon_{t}\sim q_{\beta_{t},\lambda_{t},\Sigma_{t}} .
At reverse step k k , the Energy-Tweedie score estimate is s ^ t k ( y k ) = − λ t k β t k ∇ y PD ES Σ t k − 1 , β t k ( P θ ( ⋅ ∣ y k , t k ) , y k ) ≈ − λ t k N Σ t k − 1 ∑ i = 1 N ∥ y k − X ( i ) ∥ Σ t k − 1 β t k − 2 ( y k − X ( i ) ) . \widehat{s}_{t_{k}}(y_{k})=-\frac{\lambda_{t_{k}}}{\beta_{t_{k}}}\nabla_{y}^{\mathrm{PD}}\mathrm{ES}_{\Sigma_{t_{k}}^{-1},\beta_{t_{k}}}\big(P_{\theta}(\cdot\mid y_{k},t_{k}),y_{k}\big)\approx-\frac{\lambda_{t_{k}}}{N}\Sigma_{t_{k}}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|y_{k}-X^{(i)}\|_{\Sigma_{t_{k}}^{-1}}^{\beta_{t_{k}}-2}(y_{k}-X^{(i)}). (32) Note that it is not strictly necessary that the estimator P θ ( ⋅ ∣ Y , t ) P_{\theta}(\cdot\mid Y,t) was trained under the induced scoring rule — any form of distributional matching would suffice. However, it must have been trained over the range of ( β t , λ t , Σ t ) \left(\beta_{t},\lambda_{t},\Sigma_{t}\right) it is expected to see at sampling time.
Despite this, matching the energy score to the noise-parameter path at training might prove useful: for example, when β ≈ 1 \beta\approx 1 , the energy score will encourage robustness to extreme values, which are likely to come up at that step due to the noising distribution. Subsequent to the preprint versions of this work, Martorella et al. [22] modify the energy-score parameters of a scoring-rule diffusion model along the diffusion schedule while keeping isotropic Gaussian noise, and report improved few-step generation at ImageNet scale, in particular when only β t \beta_{t} varies.
Plugging the Energy-Tweedie score into an annealed Langevin sampler thus yields a diffusion-style procedure over a noise-parameter path .
In the isotropic Gaussian case, this can be viewed through the usual reverse-SDE interpretation from “ordinary diffusion” [ 39 ] as the Energy-Tweedie identity (Eq. 21 ) simplifies to the Tweedie identity; in the general case, it should be viewed as score-based MCMC over a sequence of intermediate targets as was the original perspective in NCSN/SMLD [ 37 ] .
The use of scoring rules for diffusion-like generative modeling has been explored in Reverse Markov Learning [ 36 ] and Distributional Diffusion Models with Scoring Rules [ 8 ] .
In contrast to the procedure above, neither runs a score-based
generation procedure; RML uses ancestral sampling and is thus roughly analogous to DDPM, while De Bortoli et al. [8] employ an approach analogous to DDIM using the learned full posterior p ⁡ ( x 0 ∣ x t ) p(x_{0}\mid x_{t}) . Our proposed approach thus provides the missing score-based perspective analogous to NCSN/SMLD. We reuse the Eight-Gaussians posterior model of Sec. 5.1 and train a corresponding one on MNIST [ 20 ] , both conditioned on η = ( β , λ , σ , u , v ) \eta=(\beta,\lambda,\sigma,u,v) .
Through Eq. 32 , each
model supplies the Energy-Tweedie score anywhere in the noise-parameter space, and hence along an arbitrary noise-parameter path t ↦ ( β t , λ t , Σ t ) t\mapsto(\beta_{t},\lambda_{t},\Sigma_{t}) instead of a single scalar noise schedule.
Figure 1 displays the learned score field for the Eight-Gaussians data as each
parameter is varied in turn; in particular, the mean-seeking Gaussian field ( β = 2 \beta=2 ) concentrates mass toward the ring, while for β < 2 \beta<2 the field tends toward the geometric-median regime and targets the mixture centers directly, as discussed in Sec. 3.1 . The displayed base parameter combination is ( β , λ , ρ , u , v ) = ( 1.4 , 1.8 , 0.57 , 0 , 0.6 ) (\beta,\lambda,\rho,u,v)=(1.4,1.8,0.57,0,0.6) ( ρ \rho is the reference RMS of Eq. 42 ). Figure 1 : Energy-Tweedie score fields from the five-parameter Eight-Gaussians posterior model as each noise parameter is varied. The colorbar represents the score magnitude (log scale). For generation, we run annealed Langevin over four reverse paths through the noise-parameter space, matched in noise RMS: an isotropic fixed-tail reference, a fixed non-diagonal covariance, a covariance completing a full rotation, and a joint path along which all five parameters vary simultaneously. The path definitions, setup and more results are given in App. A.6.1 . The same construction carries over to MNIST. We examine five paths with a common noise magnitude: four fixed combinations of Gaussian or generalized radial law with
isotropic or non-diagonal covariance, together with a jointly varying path that transforms Gaussian diffusion continuously into a heavy-tailed anisotropic one (definitions in App. A.6.2 ).
Figure 2 shows unselected samples from all five
paths, each producing recognizable, class-diverse digits.
On quantitative metrics (Table 13 in App. A.6.2 ), the generalized/non-diagonal path attains the best point estimates on all metrics. As the model, noise magnitude schedule and sampling budget are shared, this comparison shows that the noise-parameter path, enabled by the Energy-Tweedie identity , is a consequential modeling choice. Additional results are presented in App. A.6 . Figure 2 : Unselected MNIST samples from the Gaussian/isotropic, Gaussian/non-diagonal,
generalized/isotropic, generalized/non-diagonal, and joint paths (left to right). 6 Related Work Classical work on Tweedie-type identities and empirical Bayes goes back to Robbins [29] and refinements such as Efron [10] .
Score matching [ 15 ; 38 ] provides a way to estimate score fields and forms the basis of modern score-based generative modeling [ 39 ] .
Self-supervised denoising handles unknown noise mainly either through risk surrogates or through Tweedie-based score extraction. SURE-type methods estimate or optimize denoising risk under Gaussian noise, with UNSURE relaxing the need to know the noise level [ 40 ; 43 ] . Noise2Score and Noise-Distribution Adaptive Denoising instead estimate the noisy marginal score using denoising autoencoder machinery and plug it into classic Tweedie formulas [ 17 ; 18 ] . The use of denoising autoencoders provides a contrast to recent work on energy-score-based autoencoders [ 34 ] , for which non-asymptotic score alignment has been established [ 19 ] , albeit in a different setting. Estimation of unknown noise parameters has typically been indirect, relying on surrogates such as reconstructed-image regularity (cf. Sec. 5.2 ).
In addition to the scoring-rule-based diffusion approaches discussed in Sec. 5.3 , heavy-tailed diffusion methods replace Gaussian perturbations with richer noising processes: Yoon et al. [47] use isotropic α \alpha -stable Lévy processes and fractional denoising score matching, Shariatian et al. [33] give a DDPM-style construction with α \alpha -stable noise, and Pandey et al. [26] use Student- t t perturbations to model heavy-tailed data. These works modify the forward noising process or reverse sampler, whereas our contribution is a score identity. For Student- t t noise in particular, Cor. 4.4 supplies the corresponding identity and matched kernel score.
Concurrent work also links generative modeling to score-induced geometry: Energy Matching [ 1 ] uses an interaction term to ensure diversity (conceptually similar to the energy score), and an optimal-transport term to encourage direct paths to the data manifold, which we observe for β ≈ 1 \beta\approx 1 ; dual score matching [ 14 ] learns normalized image energies whose spatial gradient is the score; and the Information–Estimation Metric [ 25 ] builds data-dependent distances by comparing denoising-error or score fields over Gaussian noise scales. In our case the metric is tied to the additive noise law, and the identity singles out a “mean β \beta -energy” (App. A.7.1 ). 7 Discussion This work generalizes the Gaussian Tweedie correspondence from a posterior-mean identity to a distributional identity that holds for any additive Gibbs noise. The main object is the denoising posterior viewed through a kernel score induced by the noise potential.
The resulting Energy-Tweedie identity then gives the score of the noisy marginal as the path-derivative of this matched scoring rule, with the noise potential alone determining propriety. The classical results are recovered in the Gaussian case.
The identity gives a posterior-sample route to score estimation, turns the estimation of unknown noise parameters (for any Gibbs noise) into an M-estimation problem that moves beyond estimating a scalar noise level, and provides the missing score-based view of “distributional” diffusion approaches, where the process can be non-Gaussian, and the
noise-parameter schedule becomes a path chosen freely at sampling time.
These consequences also suggest promising additional uses. In inverse problems [ 7 ] , many approaches use the score of noisy distributions, which Energy-Tweedie provides without the Gaussian assumption.
Similarly, in test-time denoising under noise distribution shift, the discrepancy in Eq. 27 could be used to estimate candidate parameters.
While the identity is general, we have focused on the generalized Gaussian family for the examples. The theory for parameter estimation currently assumes that the posterior is correctly specified and that the auxiliary score estimator is sufficiently accurate; relaxing these could be a future direction. On the diffusion side, scaling to competitive large-scale image-generation benchmarks remains open. A.1 Proofs of Section 2 A.1.1 Proof of the Generalized Tweedie Formula The Fisher identity is given in Cappé et al. [5] as: ∇ θ ℓ ​ ( θ ) | θ = θ ′ = ∫ ∇ θ ​ log ​ f ​ ( x , θ ) | θ = θ ′ ​ p ​ ( x , θ ′ ) ​ λ ​ ( 𝑑 x ) , \left.\nabla_{\theta}\ell(\theta)\right|_{\theta=\theta^{\prime}}=\left.\int\nabla_{\theta}\log f(x;\theta)\right|_{\theta=\theta^{\prime}}p\left(x;\theta^{\prime}\right)\lambda(dx), (33) with f ⁡ ( x , θ ) f(x;\theta) being an unnormalized density, L ⁡ ( θ ) L(\theta) the normalization constant: L ⁡ ( θ ) = ∫ f ⁡ ( x , θ ) ​ λ ​ ( 𝑑 x ) , L(\theta)=\int f(x;\theta)\lambda(dx), ℓ ⁡ ( θ ) \ell(\theta) the log-likelihood: ℓ ⁡ ( θ ) = log ⁡ L ⁡ ( θ ) , \ell(\theta)=\log L(\theta), and p ⁡ ( x , θ ) = f ⁡ ( x , θ ) / L ⁡ ( θ ) . p(x;\theta)=f(x;\theta)/L(\theta). As Eq. 33 is an analytic identity, we can perform the following symbolic substitution: θ → y , θ ′ → y ′ \theta\rightarrow y,\theta^{\prime}\rightarrow y^{\prime} . Without restricting ourselves to location family noise, we can use a general noising kernel k ⁡ ( y | x ) k(y|x) . In our
notation, we have the following: f ⁡ ( x , y ) = k ⁡ ( y ∣ x ) ​ p ​ ( x ) = p X , Y ​ ( x , y ) , f(x;y)=k(y\mid x)\,p(x)=p_{X,Y}(x,y), L ⁡ ( y ) = ∫ k ⁡ ( y ∣ x ) ​ p ​ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 x = m ⁡ ( y ) , L(y)=\int k(y\mid x)\,p(x)\,dx=m(y), and p ⁡ ( x , y ) = k ⁡ ( y ∣ x ) ​ p ​ ( x ) / m ⁡ ( y ) = p X | Y = y ​ ( x ) . p(x;y)=k(y\mid x)\,p(x)\,/\,m(y)=p_{X\mid Y=y}(x). Plugging this into the Fisher identity 33 , we have: ∇ y ℓ ​ ( y ) | y = y ′ = ∫ ∇ y [ log ⁡ k ⁡ ( y | x ) + log ⁡ p ⁡ ( x ) ≠ f ⁡ ( y ) ] | y = y ′ ​ k ⁡ ( y ′ ∣ x ) ​ p ​ ( x ) m ⁡ ( y ′ ) ⏟ p ⁡ ( x ∣ Y = y ′ ) ​ 𝑑 x \left.\nabla_{y}\ell(y)\right|_{y=y^{\prime}}=\left.\int\nabla_{y}\left[\log k(y|x)+\underset{\neq f(y)}{\cancel{\log p(x)}}\right]\right|_{y=y^{\prime}}\underbrace{\frac{k(y^{\prime}\mid x)\,p(x)}{m(y^{\prime})}}_{p(x\mid Y=y^{\prime})}~dx Switching y y and y ′ y^{\prime} around and noting ∇ y ℓ ​ ( y ) = s m ​ ( y ) \nabla_{y}\ell(y)=s_{m}(y) , we thus have: s m ( y ) = 𝔼 X ∼ P ( ⋅ ∣ Y = y ) [ ∇ y log k ( y ∣ X ) ] . s_{m}(y)\;=\;{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim P(\cdot\mid Y=y)}\left[\;\nabla_{y}\log k(y\mid X)\right]. (34) For the location family, we have: k ⁡ ( y ∣ X ) = q ⁡ ( y − X ) , k(y\mid X)=q(y-X), thus we recover Eq. 4 : s m ( y ) = 𝔼 X ∼ P ( ⋅ ∣ Y = y ) [ ∇ y log q ( y − X ) ] . s_{m}(y)\;=\;{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim P(\cdot\mid Y=y)}\left[\;\nabla_{y}\log q(y-X)\right]. (35) To recover the “classic” Tweedie’s formula, we plug in a multivariate Gaussian: k ⁡ ( y | x ) = q ⁡ ( y − x ) ∝ exp ⁡ [ − 1 2 ​ ( y − x ) ⊤ ​ Σ − 1 ​ ( y − x ) ] , k(y|x)=q(y-x)\propto\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}(y-x)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}(y-x)\right], we obtain: s m ( y ) = 𝔼 X ∼ P ( ⋅ ∣ Y = y ) [ ∇ y ( − 1 2 ( y − X ) ⊤ Σ − 1 ( y − X ) ) ] = 𝔼 X ∼ P ( ⋅ ∣ Y = y ) [ − Σ − 1 ( y − X ) ] s_{m}(y)\;=\;{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim P(\cdot\mid Y=y)}\left[\;\nabla_{y}\left(-\frac{1}{2}(y-X)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}(y-X)\right)\right]=\;{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim P(\cdot\mid Y=y)}\left[-\Sigma^{-1}(y-X)\right] = Σ − 1 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ X ∣ Y = y ] − Σ − 1 ​ y , =\Sigma^{-1}{\mathbb{E}}\left[X\mid Y=y\right]-\Sigma^{-1}y, hence: 𝔼 ⁡ [ X ∣ Y = y ] = y + Σ ​ s m ​ ( y ) . {\mathbb{E}}\left[X\mid Y=y\right]=y+\Sigma\,s_{m}(y). ∎ A.2 Proofs of Section 3 A.2.1 Proof of Proposition 3.2 Substituting Eq. 13 into Eq. 4 (Assumption (A2)) gives
the first equality; by (A3) the substitution is valid P ( ⋅ ∣ Y = y ) P(\cdot\mid Y=y) -almost surely,
and (A4) makes the expectation well defined. The second equality is the definition of the
path derivative (Eq. 14 ) applied to f ⁡ ( y , X ) = ℰ ⁡ ( y − X ) f(y,X)={\mathcal{E}}(y-X) .
∎ A.2.2 Derivations for the Examples The examples are special cases of q ⁡ ( u ) ∝ exp ⁡ { − V ⁡ ( ‖ u ‖ Σ − 1 ) } q(u)\propto\exp\{-V(\|u\|_{\Sigma^{-1}})\} with an elliptical potential V V .
For such, Eq. 13 becomes: ∇ y ​ log ​ q ​ ( y − X ) = − ∇ y V ​ ( ‖ y − X ‖ Σ − 1 ) = − V ′ ​ ( ‖ y − X ‖ Σ − 1 ) ‖ y − X ‖ Σ − 1 ​ Σ − 1 ​ ( y − X ) , \nabla_{y}\log q(y-X)\;=\;-\,\nabla_{y}V\!\left(\|y-X\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}\right)=-\,\frac{V^{\prime}(\|y-X\|_{\Sigma^{-1}})}{\|y-X\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}}\,\Sigma^{-1}(y-X), (36) By the definition of the path derivative (Eq. 14 ), this is: s m ​ ( y ) = − 𝔼 ⁡ [ ∇ y V ​ ( ‖ y − X ‖ Σ − 1 ) | Y = y ] = − ∇ y PD 𝔼 ​ [ V ⁡ ( ‖ y − X ‖ Σ − 1 ) | Y = y ] , s_{m}(y)=-\,{\mathbb{E}}\!\left[\nabla_{y}V\!\left(\|y-X\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}\right)\,\middle|\,Y=y\right]=-\,\nabla^{\mathrm{PD}}_{y}\,{\mathbb{E}}\!\left[V\!\left(\|y-X\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}\right)\,\middle|\,Y=y\right], (37) For Gaussian noise with V ⁡ ( r ) = r 2 / 2 V(r)=r^{2}/2 and Σ = σ 2 \Sigma=\sigma^{2} in one dimension, Eq. 37 gives s m ​ ( y ) \displaystyle s_{m}(y) = − ∇ y PD ​ 𝔼 ​ [ ( y − X ) 2 2 ​ σ 2 ∣ Y = y ] \displaystyle=-\nabla_{y}^{\mathrm{PD}}~{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{(y-X)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\mid Y=y\right] = − ∇ y PD ( y 2 2 ​ σ 2 − 2 ​ y 2 ​ σ 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ X ∣ Y = y ] + 1 2 ​ σ 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ X 2 ∣ Y = y ] ) , \displaystyle=-\nabla_{y}^{\mathrm{PD}}\left(\frac{y^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}-\frac{2y}{2\sigma^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}[X\mid Y=y]+\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}[X^{2}\mid Y=y]\right), hence s m ​ ( y ) = 𝔼 ⁡ [ X ∣ Y = y ] − y σ 2 . s_{m}(y)=\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[X\mid Y=y]-y}{\sigma^{2}}. In the multivariate Gaussian case, V ⁡ ( r ) = 1 2 ​ r 2 V(r)=\frac{1}{2}r^{2} , and s m ​ ( y ) \displaystyle s_{m}(y) = − ∇ y PD ​ 𝔼 ​ [ 1 2 ​ ‖ y − X ‖ Σ − 1 2 ∣ Y = y ] \displaystyle=-\nabla_{y}^{\mathrm{PD}}~{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|y-X\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2}\mid Y=y\right] = − ∇ y PD ( 1 2 ​ y ⊤ ​ Σ − 1 ​ y − y ⊤ ​ Σ − 1 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ X ∣ Y = y ] + 1 2 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ X ⊤ ​ Σ − 1 ​ X ∣ Y = y ] ) , \displaystyle=-\nabla_{y}^{\mathrm{PD}}\left(\frac{1}{2}y^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}y-y^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}{\mathbb{E}}[X\mid Y=y]+\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}}[X^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}X\mid Y=y]\right), so s m ​ ( y ) = Σ − 1 ​ ( 𝔼 ⁡ [ X ∣ Y = y ] − y ) . s_{m}(y)=\Sigma^{-1}\left({\mathbb{E}}[X\mid Y=y]-y\right). For multivariate Laplace noise, q ⁡ ( u ) ∝ exp ⁡ ( − λ ​ ‖ u ‖ Σ − 1 ) q(u)\propto\exp(-\lambda\|u\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}) , so V ⁡ ( r ) = λ ​ r V(r)=\lambda r . magnitude, giving the median-seeking interpretation of Sec. 3.1 . A.2.3 Proof of Lemma 17 First, (A1) holds: whitening ( u = Σ 1 / 2 ​ v u=\Sigma^{1/2}v ) and polar coordinates reduce the normalizing

By the Tweedie identity for the elliptical distributions (Eq. 37 ), we have: s m ​ ( y ) = − ∇ y PD 𝔼 ​ [ λ β ​ ‖ y − X ‖ Σ − 1 β ∣ Y = y ] = 𝔼 ⁡ [ − λ ​ ‖ y − X ‖ Σ − 1 β − 2 ​ Σ − 1 ​ ( y − X ) ∣ Y = y ] , s_{m}(y)=-\nabla_{y}^{\mathrm{PD}}~{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{\lambda}{\beta}\|y-X\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta}\mid Y=y\right]={\mathbb{E}}\left[-\lambda\,\|y-X\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta-2}~\Sigma^{-1}\,(y-X)\mid Y=y\right], hence: s m ​ ( y ) = − λ ​ Σ − 1 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ ‖ y − X ‖ Σ − 1 β − 2 ​ ( y − X ) ∣ Y = y ] . s_{m}(y)=-\lambda~\Sigma^{-1}\,{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|y-X\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta-2}~(y-X)\mid Y=y\right]. ∎ Integrability: We verify integrability condition (A4) of Prop. 3.2 ; we note that as the field
behaves like ‖ y − X ‖ β − 1 \|y-X\|^{\beta-1} , we observe three regimes: • β > 1 \beta>1 : no singularity at the diagonal X = y X=y ; the field goes to 0 0 . • β = 1 \beta=1 : bounded off the diagonal; the only issue is pointwise non-differentiability at X = y X=y , which is handled by (A3). • 0 < β < 1 0<\beta<1 : singular at the diagonal, the question is whether that singularity is locally integrable
under the posterior. For the last case: at the single point X = y X=y , the vector field may be undefined; throughout, it may be assigned an arbitrary value there,
since under the posterior it has probability zero.
Next, if the posterior density p ⁡ ( x ∣ Y = y ) p(x\mid Y=y) is bounded,

has a locally bounded density at y y as q q is continuous and finite at 0. A.3 Proofs of Section 4 A.3.1 Proof of Proposition 4.2 The claims follow from existing results: • CND ⇒ \Rightarrow proper is given by Theorem 4 in Gneiting and Raftery [12] . • Proper ⇒ \Rightarrow CND: this is stated in Sect. 5.2 of Gneiting and Raftery [12] , citing Theorem 1 in Székely and Rizzo [41] . • Strictly proper ⇔ \Leftrightarrow strong negative type: combining Eqs. (29) and (31) in Gneiting and Raftery [12] , the expected score gap under the true law Q Q is, in our negative
orientation: 𝔼 W ∼ Q ​ [ S ρ ​ ( P , W ) ] − 𝔼 W ∼ Q ​ [ S ρ ​ ( Q , W ) ] = 1 2 ​ D ρ ​ ( P , Q ) . {\mathbb{E}}_{W\sim Q}[S_{\rho}(P,W)]-{\mathbb{E}}_{W\sim Q}[S_{\rho}(Q,W)]=\tfrac{1}{2}\,D_{\rho}(P,Q). The gap is strictly positive for all P ≠ Q P\neq Q exactly when D ρ ​ ( P , Q ) > 0 D_{\rho}(P,Q)>0 for all P ≠ Q P\neq Q ,
which is the definition of strong negative type (Def. 28 in Sejdinovic et al. [32] ).
