Next, a CND ρ \rho with ρ ⁡ ( z , z ) = 0 \rho(z,z)=0 admits a Hilbert-space representation ρ ⁡ ( z , z ′ ) = ‖ φ ⁡ ( z ) − φ ⁡ ( z ′ ) ‖ ℋ 2 \rho(z,z^{\prime})=\|\varphi(z)-\varphi(z^{\prime})\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2} (Prop. 3 in Sejdinovic et al. [32] ),
so ρ 1 / 2 \rho^{1/2} obeys the triangle inequality and ρ ⁡ ( X , X ′ ) ≤ 2 ​ ρ ​ ( X , y ) + 2 ​ ρ ​ ( y , X ′ ) \rho(X,X^{\prime})\leq 2\,\rho(X,y)+2\,\rho(y,X^{\prime}) for posterior draws X , X ′ X,X^{\prime} .
Taking expectations over X , X ′ ∼ iid P ( ⋅ ∣ Y = y ) X,X^{\prime}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}P(\cdot\mid Y=y) and using the above: 𝔼 ⁡ [ ρ ⁡ ( X , X ′ ) ∣ Y = y ] ≤ 4 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ ρ ⁡ ( X , y ) ∣ Y = y ] < ∞ . {\mathbb{E}}\left[\rho(X,X^{\prime})\mid Y=y\right]\leq 4\,{\mathbb{E}}\left[\rho(X,y)\mid Y=y\right]<\infty. ∎ A.3.2 Proofs of Cors. 4.3 and 4.4 Proof of the Mahalanobis energy score propriety As the Mahalanobis distance ∥ ⋅ ∥ Σ − 1 \|\cdot\|_{\Sigma^{-1}} appears in both corollaries,
we wish to show that the β \beta -th power of the Mahalanobis distance d Σ − 1 ​ ( z i , z j ) = ‖ z i − z j ‖ Σ − 1 = ( z i − z j ) ⊤ ​ Σ − 1 ​ ( z i − z j ) d_{\Sigma^{-1}}(z_{i},z_{j})=\|z_{i}-z_{j}\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}=\sqrt{(z_{i}-z_{j})^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}(z_{i}-z_{j})} is a conditionally negative definite (CND) kernel, as well as develop some general machinery.
We assume Σ \Sigma to be a symmetric positive definite (PD) matrix Σ ≻ 0 \Sigma\succ 0 , so Σ − 1 \Sigma^{-1} exists and is symmetric PD. A real-valued function g g on ℝ d × ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d} is said to be a conditionally negative definite (CND) kernel if it is (i)
symmetric in its arguments and (ii) ∑ i = 1 n ∑ j = 1 n a i ​ a j ​ g ​ ( z i , z j ) ≤ 0 \sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{i}a_{j}g\left(z_{i},z_{j}\right)\leq 0 for all
positive integers n n , all z 1 , … , z n z_{1},\ldots,z_{n} , and all a 1 , … , a n ∈ ℝ a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{R} that sum to 0 0 (this
is the “conditionally” part). To show symmetry, we can simply write: g ⁡ ( z j , z i ) \displaystyle g(z_{j},z_{i}) = ∥ z j − z i ∥ Σ − 1 β = ( ⟨ Σ − 1 / 2 ( z j − z i ) , Σ − 1 / 2 ( z j − z i ) ⟩ ) β 2 \displaystyle=\|z_{j}-z_{i}\|^{\beta}_{\Sigma^{-1}}=\left(\langle\Sigma^{-1/2}(z_{j}-z_{i}),\Sigma^{-1/2}(z_{j}-z_{i})\rangle\right)^{\tfrac{\beta}{2}} = ( ⟨ Σ − 1 / 2 z j , Σ − 1 / 2 z j ⟩ − 2 ⟨ Σ − 1 / 2 z j , Σ − 1 / 2 z i ⟩ + ⟨ Σ − 1 / 2 z i , Σ − 1 / 2 z i ⟩ ) β 2 \displaystyle=\left(\langle\Sigma^{-1/2}z_{j},\Sigma^{-1/2}z_{j}\rangle-2\langle\Sigma^{-1/2}z_{j},\Sigma^{-1/2}z_{i}\rangle+\langle\Sigma^{-1/2}z_{i},\Sigma^{-1/2}z_{i}\rangle\right)^{\tfrac{\beta}{2}} = ‖ z i − z j ‖ Σ − 1 β = g ⁡ ( z i , z j ) \displaystyle=\|z_{i}-z_{j}\|^{\beta}_{\Sigma^{-1}}=g(z_{i},z_{j}) β = 2 \beta=2 case: For the second requirement, let’s begin with the β = 2 \beta=2 case: ∑ i = 1 n ∑ j = 1 n a i a j g ( z j , z i ) = ∑ i = 1 n a i ∑ j = 1 n a j ( ∥ Σ − 1 / 2 z i ∥ 2 − 2 ⟨ Σ − 1 / 2 z j , Σ − 1 / 2 z i ⟩ + ∥ Σ − 1 / 2 z j ∥ 2 ) = \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{i}a_{j}~g(z_{j},z_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\big(\|\Sigma^{-1/2}z_{i}\|^{2}-2\langle\Sigma^{-1/2}z_{j},\Sigma^{-1/2}z_{i}\rangle+\|\Sigma^{-1/2}z_{j}\|^{2}\big)= ∑ i = 1 n a i ∑ j = 1 n a j ( ∥ Σ − 1 / 2 z i ∥ 2 + ∥ Σ − 1 / 2 z j ∥ 2 ) ⏟ A − 2 ​ ∑ i = 1 n a i ​ ∑ j = 1 n a j ​ z j ⊤ ​ Σ − 1 ​ z i ⏟ B ≤ ? 0 \displaystyle\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\big(\|\Sigma^{-1/2}z_{i}\|^{2}+\|\Sigma^{-1/2}z_{j}\|^{2}\big)}_{A}-\underbrace{2\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}z_{j}^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}z_{i}}_{B}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{\leq}}0 For the terms in A, we notice that: ∑ i , j a i ​ a j ​ z i ⊤ ​ Σ − 1 ​ z i = ∑ i a i ​ z i ⊤ ​ Σ − 1 ​ z i ​ ∑ j a j ⏟ = 0 = 0 , \sum_{i,j}a_{i}a_{j}\,z_{i}^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}z_{i}=\sum_{i}a_{i}\,z_{i}^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}z_{i}\;\underbrace{\sum_{j}a_{j}}_{=\,0}=0, and vice-versa for the z j z_{j} norm term. Thus, we are left with the term in B: − 2 ∑ i = 1 n a i ∑ j = 1 n a j ⟨ Σ − 1 / 2 z j , Σ − 1 / 2 z i ⟩ = − 2 ( ∑ i = 1 n a i z i ) ⊤ Σ − 1 ( ∑ j = 1 n a j z j ) ≤ 0 , \displaystyle-2\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\langle\Sigma^{-1/2}z_{j},\Sigma^{-1/2}z_{i}\rangle=-2(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}z_{i})^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}(\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}z_{j})\leq 0, Since Σ − 1 \Sigma^{-1} is PD, the above quadratic form is nonnegative and the expression is ≤ 0 \leq 0 after multiplication by -2.
Together with Theorem 4 in Gneiting and Raftery [12] , this gives propriety of the scoring rule
(Eq. 9 ) at β = 2 \beta=2 . ∎ General case — β ∈ ( 0 , 2 ) \beta\in(0,2) : First of all, we note that the Mahalanobis norm is not a CND kernel for β > 2 \beta>2 : Let ( a 1 , a 2 , a 3 ) = ( 1 , 1 , − 2 ) (a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})=(1,1,-2) and z 1 = z z_{1}=z , z 2 = w z_{2}=w , z 3 = z ¯ = Δ z + w 2 z_{3}=\bar{z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\tfrac{z+w}{2} , so ∑ i a i = 0 \sum_{i}a_{i}=0 . Since z ¯ \bar{z} is the midpoint of z z and w w , the three nonzero pairwise distances are ‖ z 1 − z 2 ‖ Σ − 1 = ‖ z − w ‖ Σ − 1 , ‖ z 1 − z 3 ‖ Σ − 1 = ‖ z 2 − z 3 ‖ Σ − 1 = 1 2 ​ ‖ z − w ‖ Σ − 1 . \|z_{1}-z_{2}\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}=\|z-w\|_{\Sigma^{-1}},\qquad\|z_{1}-z_{3}\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}=\|z_{2}-z_{3}\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}=\tfrac{1}{2}\|z-w\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}. The diagonal terms vanish, and by symmetry each ( i , j ) (i,j) term can be paired with the corresponding ( j , i ) (j,i) term. Therefore, ∑ i = 1 3 ∑ j = 1 3 a i ​ a j ​ ‖ z i − z j ‖ Σ − 1 β \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{3}\sum_{j=1}^{3}a_{i}a_{j}\,\|z_{i}-z_{j}\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta} = 2 ​ [ a 1 ​ a 2 ​ ‖ z 1 − z 2 ‖ Σ − 1 β + a 1 ​ a 3 ​ ‖ z 1 − z 3 ‖ Σ − 1 β + a 2 ​ a 3 ​ ‖ z 2 − z 3 ‖ Σ − 1 β ] \displaystyle=2\left[a_{1}a_{2}\|z_{1}-z_{2}\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta}+a_{1}a_{3}\|z_{1}-z_{3}\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta}+a_{2}a_{3}\|z_{2}-z_{3}\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta}\right] = 2 ​ [ ‖ z − w ‖ Σ − 1 β − 2 ​ ( 1 2 ​ ‖ z − w ‖ Σ − 1 ) β − 2 ​ ( 1 2 ​ ‖ z − w ‖ Σ − 1 ) β ] \displaystyle=2\left[\|z-w\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta}-2\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\|z-w\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}\right)^{\beta}-2\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\|z-w\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}\right)^{\beta}\right] = 2 ​ ‖ z − w ‖ Σ − 1 β ​ ( 1 − 2 2 − β ) . \displaystyle=2\|z-w\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta}\left(1-2^{2-\beta}\right). The CND inequality would require 1 − 2 2 − β ≤ 0 1-2^{2-\beta}\leq 0 , equivalently β ≤ 2 \beta\leq 2 . Thus, for β > 2 \beta>2 , the sum is strictly positive whenever z ≠ w z\neq w , which violates the CND inequality for every Σ ≻ 0 \Sigma\succ 0 . ∎ Now, we tackle the general case for β ∈ ( 0 , 2 ) \beta\in(0,2) :
For this, we reproduce a classical result due to Schoenberg [31] , using a more modern reference found
in Berg [2] . We will be using the concept of a Bernstein function, which is a 𝒞 ∞ {\mathcal{C}}^{\infty} function whose derivative is
completely monotone. Such functions φ \varphi admit the Levy-Khinchin representation (Def. 5.1 in [ 2 ] ): φ ⁡ ( t ) = a + b ​ t + ∫ 0 ∞ ( 1 − e − t ​ s ) ​ μ ​ ( 𝑑 s ) , \varphi(t)\;=\;a+b\,t+\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(1-e^{-ts}\big)\,\mu(ds), for some a , b ≥ 0 a,b\geq 0 and a positive measure μ \mu on ( 0 , ∞ ) (0,\infty) . Further, note that φ ⁡ ( t ) = t α \varphi(t)=t^{\alpha} is Bernstein ∀ α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] \forall\alpha\in(0,1] (Example on p. 16 in [ 2 ] ). Next, we claim that the composition of a Bernstein function and a CND kernel is a CND kernel. To see this, use the Levy-Khinchin representation. Denote for the CND kernel g ⁡ ( z i , z j ) = Δ g i ​ j g(z_{i},z_{j})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}g_{ij} . By the
Levy-Khinchin representation: ∑ i , j a i ​ a j ​ φ ∘ g i ​ j = ∑ i , j a i ​ a j ​ φ ​ ( g i ​ j ) = a ​ ∑ i , j a i ​ a j + b ​ ∑ i , j a i ​ a j ​ g i ​ j + ∫ 0 ∞ ∑ i , j a i ​ a j ​ ( 1 − e − s ​ g i ​ j ) ​ μ ​ ( 𝑑 s ) . \sum_{i,j}a_{i}a_{j}~\varphi\circ g_{ij}=\sum_{i,j}a_{i}a_{j}\,\varphi\big(g_{ij}\big)=a\sum_{i,j}a_{i}a_{j}+b\sum_{i,j}a_{i}a_{j}\,g_{ij}+\int_{0}^{\infty}\sum_{i,j}a_{i}a_{j}\big(1-e^{-sg_{ij}}\big)\,\mu(ds). (38) Next, we use Schoenberg’s theorem (Thm. 2.2 in [ 2 ] ): A function g g is CND ⇔ \Leftrightarrow exp ⁡ ( − s ​ g ) \exp(-sg) is positive definite for all s > 0 s>0 . So, denoting e − s ​ g i ​ j e^{-sg_{ij}} as the PD kernel K s ​ ( z i , z j ) K_{s}(z_{i},z_{j}) , we have for the terms in
Eq. 38 : • a ​ ∑ i , j a i ​ a j = a ​ ∑ i a i ⏟ = 0 ​ ∑ j a j ⏟ = 0 ≤ 0 a\sum_{i,j}a_{i}a_{j}=a\underbrace{\sum_{i}a_{i}}_{=0}\underbrace{\sum_{j}a_{j}}_{=0}\leq 0 • b ​ ∑ i , j a i ​ a j ​ g i ​ j ≤ 0 b\sum_{i,j}a_{i}a_{j}g_{ij}\leq 0 since b ≥ 0 b\geq 0 and g g is CND. • ∑ i , j a i ​ a j ​ ( 1 − K s ​ ( z i , z j ) ) = ( ∑ i a i ) 2 ⏟ = 0 − ∑ i , j a i ​ a j ​ K s ​ ( z i , z j ) ≤ 0 . \sum_{i,j}a_{i}a_{j}\big(1-K_{s}(z_{i},z_{j})\big)=\underbrace{\Big(\sum_{i}a_{i}\Big)^{2}}_{=\,0}\;-\;\sum_{i,j}a_{i}a_{j}K_{s}(z_{i},z_{j})\;\leq\;0. Integration of the nonpositive term above ( ∀ s \forall s ) with respect to the positive measure μ \mu is thus nonpositive.
We thus have that a composition of a Bernstein function and a CND kernel is a CND kernel. ∎ We will first use this to prove that the Mahalanobis norm is a CND kernel for β ∈ ( 0 , 2 ) \beta\in(0,2) , and thus the corresponding energy score is a proper scoring rule. Let α = β / 2 ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha=\beta/2\in(0,1) , and denote k β = ( k 2 ) α k_{\beta}=(k_{2})^{\alpha} , and k 2 ​ ( z , w ) = ‖ L ​ z − L ​ w ‖ 2 2 = ‖ z − w ‖ Σ − 1 2 , k_{2}(z,w)\;=\;\|Lz-Lw\|_{2}^{2}=\|z-w\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2}, where we are using the Cholesky decomposition Σ − 1 = L ⊤ ​ L \Sigma^{-1}=L^{\top}L . Using Schoenberg’s theorem (see above) again, k 2 k_{2} is CND as the corresponding factor e − s ​ ‖ u − u ′ ‖ 2 2 e^{-s\|u-u^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}} is the well-known PD Gaussian kernel for u = L ​ z u=Lz and u ′ = L ​ w u^{\prime}=Lw . Thus we have ‖ z − w ‖ Σ − 1 β = k β = t α ∘ k 2 \|z-w\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta}=k_{\beta}=t^{\alpha}\circ k_{2} is CND by the composition rule for β ∈ ( 0 , 2 ) \beta\in(0,2) .
Then, following Theorem 4 in Gneiting and Raftery [12] , the corresponding scoring rule
(Eq. 9 ) is proper. ∎ For strict propriety, we reference Theorem 5 in Gneiting and Raftery [12] . It requires the scoring rule to be expressible as: S ⁡ ( P , w ) = 𝔼 Z ∼ P ​ [ ψ ⁡ ( ‖ Z − w ‖ 2 2 ) ] − 1 2 ​ 𝔼 Z , Z ′ ​ ∼ iid ​ P ​ [ ψ ⁡ ( ‖ Z − Z ′ ‖ 2 2 ) ] S(P,w)={\mathbb{E}}_{Z\sim P}[\psi\left(\|Z-w\|_{2}^{2}\right)]-\tfrac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}}_{Z,Z^{\prime}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}P}[\psi\left(\|Z-Z^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\right)] for some ψ \psi continuous on [ 0 , ∞ ) [0,\infty) and ψ ′ \psi^{\prime} completely monotone and not constant. We have, for T ⁡ ( z ) = L ​ z T(z)=Lz : ‖ z − w ‖ Σ − 1 β = ‖ T ⁡ ( z ) − T ⁡ ( w ) ‖ 2 β = ψ ⁡ ( ‖ T ⁡ ( z ) − T ⁡ ( w ) ‖ 2 2 ) , ψ ⁡ ( t ) = t β / 2 \|z-w\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta}=\|T(z)-T(w)\|_{2}^{\beta}=\psi\left(\|T(z)-T(w)\|_{2}^{2}\right),\quad\psi(t)=t^{\beta/2} ψ ⁡ ( t ) = t β / 2 \psi(t)=t^{\beta/2} is the example given for Theorem 5 in Gneiting and Raftery [12] .
Since T ⁡ ( z ) = L ​ z T(z)=Lz is invertible, equality of the whitened distributions is equivalent to equality of the original ones, so strictness transfers to the originals.
Hence, the Mahalanobis energy score is, much like the ordinary energy score, strictly proper for β ∈ ( 0 , 2 ) \beta\in(0,2) . ∎ β = 2 \beta=2 case for the generalized Gaussian We show the collapse of the generalized energy distance D ρ D_{\rho} as stated in Cor. 4.3 .
At β = 2 \beta=2 the induced discrepancy is ρ ⁡ ( z , w ) = ‖ z − w ‖ Σ − 1 2 \rho(z,w)=\|z-w\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2} ; P P , Q Q have finite second moments throughout (the finite ρ \rho -moment class at β = 2 \beta=2 ). We use two standard facts. A positive definite reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) kernel k k is said to generate the discrepancy ρ \rho if ρ ⁡ ( z , w ) = k ⁡ ( z , z ) + k ⁡ ( w , w ) − 2 ​ k ​ ( z , w ) . \rho(z,w)=k(z,z)+k(w,w)-2\,k(z,w). First, Theorem 22 in Sejdinovic et al. [32] states that for any generating RKHS kernel, the generalized energy distance equals twice the squared maximum mean
discrepancy (MMD): D ρ ​ ( P , Q ) = 2 ​ MMD k 2 ​ ( P , Q ) . D_{\rho}(P,Q)=2\,\mathrm{MMD}_{k}^{2}(P,Q). Second, the MMD is the distance between the kernel mean embeddings μ P , μ Q \mu_{P},\mu_{Q} of the two
distributions in the RKHS ℋ k \mathcal{H}_{k} (Lemma 4 in Gretton et al. [13] ): MMD k ​ ( P , Q ) = ‖ μ P − μ Q ‖ ℋ k , \mathrm{MMD}_{k}(P,Q)=\|\mu_{P}-\mu_{Q}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}, where a kernel mean embedding is defined as μ P = 𝔼 Z ∼ P ​ [ k ⁡ ( Z , ⋅ ) ] . \mu_{P}={\mathbb{E}}_{Z\sim P}\left[k(Z,\cdot)\right]. The squared Mahalanobis distance is generated by the linear kernel k ⁡ ( z , w ) = z ⊤ ​ Σ − 1 ​ w k(z,w)=z^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}w : k ⁡ ( z , z ) + k ⁡ ( w , w ) − 2 ​ k ​ ( z , w ) = z ⊤ ​ Σ − 1 ​ z + w ⊤ ​ Σ − 1 ​ w − 2 ​ z ⊤ ​ Σ − 1 ​ w = ‖ z − w ‖ Σ − 1 2 . k(z,z)+k(w,w)-2\,k(z,w)=z^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}z+w^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}w-2\,z^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}w=\|z-w\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2}. Its mean embedding is the ordinary mean, μ P = 𝔼 Z ∼ P ​ [ Z ] \mu_{P}={\mathbb{E}}_{Z\sim P}[Z] , and the RKHS norm reduces to ∥ ⋅ ∥ Σ − 1 \|\cdot\|_{\Sigma^{-1}} . Combining the two facts, the discrepancy collapses to a difference of means: D ρ ​ ( P , Q ) = 2 ​ ‖ 𝔼 Z ∼ P ​ [ Z ] − 𝔼 W ∼ Q ​ [ W ] ‖ Σ − 1 2 . D_{\rho}(P,Q)=2\,\bigl\|{\mathbb{E}}_{Z\sim P}[Z]-{\mathbb{E}}_{W\sim Q}[W]\bigr\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2}. ∎ Proof of Student-t propriety For the Student-t potential ℰ ⁡ ( u ) = log ⁡ ( 1 + ‖ u ‖ Σ − 1 2 ) {\mathcal{E}}(u)=\log\bigl(1+\|u\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2}\bigr) , the noise density is q ℰ , c ​ ( u ) = 1 Z ℰ ​ ( c ) ​ e − c ​ ℰ ​ ( u ) = 1 Z ℰ ​ ( c ) ​ ( 1 + ‖ u ‖ Σ − 1 2 ) − c q_{{\mathcal{E}},c}(u)\,=\frac{1}{Z_{\mathcal{E}}(c)}\,e^{-c\,{\mathcal{E}}(u)}\,=\,\frac{1}{Z_{\mathcal{E}}(c)}\,(1+\|u\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2})^{-c} for c ∈ Λ ℰ = ( d / 2 , ∞ ) c\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{E}}=(d/2,\infty) . Setting u = y − X u=y-X , the matched kernel score is: S ρ ​ ( P , y ) = 𝔼 X ∼ P ​ [ log ⁡ ( 1 + ‖ y − X ‖ Σ − 1 2 ) ] − 1 2 ​ 𝔼 X , X ′ ​ ∼ iid ​ P ​ [ log ⁡ ( 1 + ‖ X − X ′ ‖ Σ − 1 2 ) ] . S_{\rho}(P,y)\;=\;\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P}\!\left[\log\bigl(1+\|y-X\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2}\bigr)\right]\;-\;\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{E}_{X,X^{\prime}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}P}\!\left[\log\bigl(1+\|X-X^{\prime}\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2}\bigr)\right]. (39) For propriety, we follow the same approach as above with the composition rule: with k 2 ​ ( z , w ) = ‖ z − w ‖ Σ − 1 2 k_{2}(z,w)=\|z-w\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2} CND as above,
we have: ρ ⁡ ( z , w ) = log ⁡ ( 1 + ‖ z − w ‖ Σ − 1 2 ) = φ ∘ k 2 , φ ⁡ ( t ) = log ⁡ ( 1 + t ) . \rho(z,w)\;=\;\log\bigl(1+\|z-w\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2}\bigr)\;=\;\varphi\circ k_{2},\quad\varphi(t)=\log(1+t). φ \varphi is Bernstein: it is smooth and nonnegative on [ 0 , ∞ ) [0,\infty) , and φ ′ ​ ( t ) = ( 1 + t ) − 1 \varphi^{\prime}(t)=(1+t)^{-1} is completely
monotone, since ( − 1 ) n ​ φ ( n + 1 ) ​ ( t ) = n ! ​ ( 1 + t ) − ( n + 1 ) ≥ 0 (-1)^{n}\varphi^{(n+1)}(t)=n!\,(1+t)^{-(n+1)}\geq 0 . Thus ρ \rho is CND by the composition
rule, and following Theorem 4 in Gneiting and Raftery [12] , the matched kernel score
(Eq. 39 ) is proper. ∎ For strict propriety, we again apply Theorem 5 in Gneiting and Raftery [12] exactly as for the Mahalanobis energy score. For T ⁡ ( z ) = L ​ z T(z)=Lz : log ⁡ ( 1 + ‖ z − w ‖ Σ − 1 2 ) = ψ ⁡ ( ‖ T ⁡ ( z ) − T ⁡ ( w ) ‖ 2 2 ) , ψ ⁡ ( t ) = log ⁡ ( 1 + t ) , \log\bigl(1+\|z-w\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2}\bigr)=\psi\left(\|T(z)-T(w)\|_{2}^{2}\right),\quad\psi(t)=\log(1+t), with ψ \psi continuous on [ 0 , ∞ ) [0,\infty) and ψ ′ \psi^{\prime} completely monotone and not constant, as verified above;
unlike for ψ ⁡ ( t ) = t β / 2 \psi(t)=t^{\beta/2} , no parameter restriction arises. Strict propriety moment restriction for Student-t In the Student-t case, the general pairwise moment requirement in Prop. 4.2 reduces to the explicit logarithmic moment condition stated in Cor. 4.4 . Using ρ ⁡ ( z , w ) = ψ ⁡ ( ‖ T ⁡ ( z ) − T ⁡ ( w ) ‖ 2 2 ) \rho(z,w)=\psi\left(\|T(z)-T(w)\|_{2}^{2}\right) as above, Theorem 5 in Gneiting and Raftery [12] gives strict propriety for
distributions P P with finite pairwise moment 𝔼 Z , Z ′ ​ ∼ iid ​ P ​ [ ρ ⁡ ( Z , Z ′ ) ] < ∞ {\mathbb{E}}_{Z,Z^{\prime}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}P}\left[\rho(Z,Z^{\prime})\right]<\infty .
Since ρ ⁡ ( z , z ′ ) = ℰ ⁡ ( z − z ′ ) \rho(z,z^{\prime})={\mathcal{E}}(z-z^{\prime}) , this pairwise condition is equivalent to 𝔼 Z , Z ′ ​ ∼ iid ​ P ​ [ ℰ ⁡ ( Z − Z ′ ) ] < ∞ {\mathbb{E}}_{Z,Z^{\prime}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}P}\left[{\mathcal{E}}(Z-Z^{\prime})\right]<\infty . We show below that this is equivalent to the stated condition 𝔼 Z ∼ P ​ [ ℰ ​ ( Z ) ] < ∞ {\mathbb{E}}_{Z\sim P}\left[{\mathcal{E}}(Z)\right]<\infty . For any u , v ∈ ℝ d u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d} , the triangle inequality and ( a + b ) 2 ≤ 2 ​ a 2 + 2 ​ b 2 (a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2} give 1 + ‖ u + v ‖ Σ − 1 2 ≤ 4 ​ ( 1 + ‖ u ‖ Σ − 1 2 ) ​ ( 1 + ‖ v ‖ Σ − 1 2 ) 1+\|u+v\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2}\leq 4\left(1+\|u\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2}\right)\left(1+\|v\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2}\right) , hence ℰ ⁡ ( u + v ) ≤ ℰ ⁡ ( u ) + ℰ ⁡ ( v ) + 2 ​ log ⁡ 2 . {\mathcal{E}}(u+v)\leq{\mathcal{E}}(u)+{\mathcal{E}}(v)+2\log 2. Taking u = z u=z , v = − z ′ v=-z^{\prime} (with ℰ {\mathcal{E}} even) gives ρ ⁡ ( z , z ′ ) ≤ ℰ ⁡ ( z ) + ℰ ⁡ ( z ′ ) + 2 ​ log ⁡ 2 \rho(z,z^{\prime})\leq{\mathcal{E}}(z)+{\mathcal{E}}(z^{\prime})+2\log 2 , so 𝔼 Z ∼ P ​ [ ℰ ​ ( Z ) ] < ∞ {\mathbb{E}}_{Z\sim P}\left[{\mathcal{E}}(Z)\right]<\infty implies 𝔼 Z , Z ′ ​ ∼ iid ​ P ​ [ ρ ⁡ ( Z , Z ′ ) ] < ∞ {\mathbb{E}}_{Z,Z^{\prime}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}P}\left[\rho(Z,Z^{\prime})\right]<\infty .
On the other hand, taking u = z − z ′ u=z-z^{\prime} , v = z ′ v=z^{\prime} gives ℰ ⁡ ( z ) ≤ ρ ⁡ ( z , z ′ ) + ℰ ⁡ ( z ′ ) + 2 ​ log ⁡ 2 . {\mathcal{E}}(z)\leq\rho(z,z^{\prime})+{\mathcal{E}}(z^{\prime})+2\log 2. For the converse direction, suppose 𝔼 Z , Z ′ ​ ∼ iid ​ P ​ [ ρ ⁡ ( Z , Z ′ ) ] < ∞ {\mathbb{E}}_{Z,Z^{\prime}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}P}[\rho(Z,Z^{\prime})]<\infty .
By Fubini’s theorem, there exists a
fixed z 0 ∈ ℝ d z_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d} such that 𝔼 Z ∼ P ​ [ ρ ⁡ ( Z , z 0 ) ] = 𝔼 Z ∼ P ​ [ ℰ ⁡ ( Z − z 0 ) ] < ∞ . {\mathbb{E}}_{Z\sim P}[\rho(Z,z_{0})]={\mathbb{E}}_{Z\sim P}[{\mathcal{E}}(Z-z_{0})]<\infty. Applying the preceding inequality to Z = ( Z − z 0 ) + z 0 Z=(Z-z_{0})+z_{0} gives 𝔼 ⁡ [ ℰ ⁡ ( Z ) ] ≤ 𝔼 ⁡ [ ℰ ⁡ ( Z − z 0 ) ] + ℰ ⁡ ( z 0 ) + 2 ​ log ​ 2 < ∞ . {\mathbb{E}}[{\mathcal{E}}(Z)]\leq{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathcal{E}}(Z-z_{0})]+{\mathcal{E}}(z_{0})+2\log 2<\infty. Thus 𝔼 Z , Z ′ ​ ∼ iid ​ P ​ [ ρ ⁡ ( Z , Z ′ ) ] < ∞ {\mathbb{E}}_{Z,Z^{\prime}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}P}\left[\rho(Z,Z^{\prime})\right]<\infty also implies 𝔼 Z ∼ P ​ [ ℰ ​ ( Z ) ] < ∞ {\mathbb{E}}_{Z\sim P}\left[{\mathcal{E}}(Z)\right]<\infty . ∎ Verification of the assumptions (A1)-(A4) for Student-t Since ∇ ℰ \nabla{\mathcal{E}} is bounded, (A2)–(A4) hold for every data distribution p p ; we verify this below. For the Student-t potential ℰ ⁡ ( u ) = log ⁡ ( 1 + ‖ u ‖ Σ − 1 2 ) {\mathcal{E}}(u)=\log(1+\|u\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2}) , (A1) is exactly the stated restriction c > d / 2 c>d/2 .
Furthermore, the gradient satisfies ∥ ∇ ℰ ( u ) ∥ = 2 ​ ‖ Σ − 1 ​ u ‖ 1 + ‖ u ‖ Σ − 1 2 ≤ 2 ∥ Σ − 1 / 2 ∥ op ‖ u ‖ Σ − 1 1 + ‖ u ‖ Σ − 1 2 ≤ ∥ Σ − 1 / 2 ∥ op . \|\nabla{\mathcal{E}}(u)\|=\frac{2\|\Sigma^{-1}u\|}{1+\|u\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2}}\leq 2\|\Sigma^{-1/2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\frac{\|u\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}}{1+\|u\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2}}\leq\|\Sigma^{-1/2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}. Thus ℰ {\mathcal{E}} is smooth and has bounded gradient, which gives (A3) and (A4) for every data distribution. It remains to check (A2). Since q ℰ , c ≤ Z ℰ ​ ( c ) − 1 q_{{\mathcal{E}},c}\leq Z_{\mathcal{E}}(c)^{-1} and ∇ q ℰ , c = − c q ℰ , c ∇ ℰ \nabla q_{{\mathcal{E}},c}=-c\,q_{{\mathcal{E}},c}\nabla{\mathcal{E}} , the gradient of the noise density is also bounded by a constant C C .
By the mean-value theorem, the difference quotients of the
noise density in any coordinate direction e i e_{i} then satisfy, for all x x and all h ≠ 0 h\neq 0 : | q ℰ , c ​ ( y + h ​ e i − x ) − q ℰ , c ​ ( y − x ) h | ≤ C , \left|\frac{q_{{\mathcal{E}},c}(y+he_{i}-x)-q_{{\mathcal{E}},c}(y-x)}{h}\right|\;\leq\;C, and the constant C C is integrable under any data distribution p p .
Now, (A2) asserts that the generalized Tweedie formula (Eq. 4 ) holds at y y ,
and the Stein score is given by s m ​ ( y ) = ∇ y ​ log ​ m ​ ( y ) = ∇ m ​ ( y ) / m ⁡ ( y ) s_{m}(y)=\nabla_{y}\log m(y)=\nabla m(y)/m(y) .
We can thus exchange the gradient with the integral in the convolution by dominated convergence: ∇ m ( y ) = ∇ y ∫ q ℰ , c ( y − x ) p ( x ) d x = ∫ ∇ q ℰ , c ( y − x ) p ( x ) d x . \nabla m(y)=\nabla_{y}\int q_{{\mathcal{E}},c}(y-x)\,p(x)\,dx=\int\nabla q_{{\mathcal{E}},c}(y-x)\,p(x)\,dx. Dividing the result by m ⁡ ( y ) m(y) and using Bayes’ rule gives: s m ​ ( y ) = 1 m ⁡ ( y ) ​ ∫ ∇ q ℰ , c ​ ( y − x ) ​ p ​ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 x = 𝔼 ⁡ [ ∇ log ⁡ q ℰ , c ​ ( y − X ) ∣ Y = y ] , s_{m}(y)=\frac{1}{m(y)}\int\nabla q_{{\mathcal{E}},c}(y-x)p(x)\,dx={\mathbb{E}}\left[\nabla\log q_{{\mathcal{E}},c}(y-X)\mid Y=y\right], which is (A2). A.4 Appendix for Score Estimation A.4.1 Bias-Variance decomposition of the Monte Carlo Score estimator Let h η ​ ( y , x ) = − λ ​ Σ − 1 ​ ‖ y − x ‖ Σ − 1 β − 2 ​ ( y − x ) h_{\eta}(y,x)=-\lambda\Sigma^{-1}\|y-x\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta-2}(y-x) (40) be the clean-conditioned score and s ^ N ​ ( y ) = 1 N ​ ∑ i = 1 N h η ​ ( y , X ( i ) ) , \widehat{s}_{N}(y)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\eta}(y,X^{(i)}), where X ( i ) ∼ iid P θ ( ⋅ ∣ y ) X^{(i)}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}P_{\theta}(\cdot\mid y) for i = 1 , … , N i=1,\ldots,N . The conditional error separates exactly as 𝔼 X ( i ) ∼ iid P θ ( ⋅ ∣ y ) [ ∥ s ^ N ( y ) − s m ( y ) ∥ 2 2 ] \displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{X^{(i)}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}P_{\theta}(\cdot\mid y)}\!\left[\|\widehat{s}_{N}(y)-s_{m}(y)\|_{2}^{2}\right] = ‖ 𝔼 X ∼ P θ ( ⋅ ∣ y ) [ h η ( y , X ) ] − s m ( y ) ‖ 2 2 \displaystyle=\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim P_{\theta}(\cdot\mid y)}[h_{\eta}(y,X)]-s_{m}(y)\right\|_{2}^{2} (41) + 1 N tr Cov X ∼ P θ ( ⋅ ∣ y ) ( h η ( y , X ) ) . \displaystyle+\frac{1}{N}\operatorname{tr}\operatorname{Cov}_{X\sim P_{\theta}(\cdot\mid y)}(h_{\eta}(y,X)). To see this, let μ θ ( y ) = 𝔼 X ∼ P θ ( ⋅ ∣ y ) [ h η ( y , X ) ] \mu_{\theta}(y)={\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim P_{\theta}(\cdot\mid y)}[h_{\eta}(y,X)] .
Conditional i.i.d. sampling gives 𝔼 X ( i ) ∼ iid P θ ( ⋅ ∣ y ) [ s ^ N ( y ) ] = μ θ ( y ) {\mathbb{E}}_{X^{(i)}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}P_{\theta}(\cdot\mid y)}[\widehat{s}_{N}(y)]=\mu_{\theta}(y) and Cov X ( i ) ∼ iid P θ ( ⋅ ∣ y ) ( s ^ N ( y ) ) = 1 N Cov X ∼ P θ ( ⋅ ∣ y ) ( h η ( y , X ) ) . \operatorname{Cov}_{X^{(i)}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}P_{\theta}(\cdot\mid y)}(\widehat{s}_{N}(y))=\tfrac{1}{N}\operatorname{Cov}_{X\sim P_{\theta}(\cdot\mid y)}(h_{\eta}(y,X)). Expanding the squared error around μ θ ​ ( y ) \mu_{\theta}(y) gives 𝔼 X ( i ) ∼ iid P θ ( ⋅ ∣ y ) [ ∥ s ^ N ( y ) − s m ( y ) ∥ 2 2 ] \displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{X^{(i)}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}P_{\theta}(\cdot\mid y)}\!\left[\|\widehat{s}_{N}(y)-s_{m}(y)\|_{2}^{2}\right] = 𝔼 X ( i ) ∼ iid P θ ( ⋅ ∣ y ) [ ∥ s ^ N ( y ) − μ θ ( y ) ∥ 2 2 ] + ∥ μ θ ( y ) − s m ( y ) ∥ 2 2 \displaystyle\quad={\mathbb{E}}_{X^{(i)}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}P_{\theta}(\cdot\mid y)}\!\Big[\|\widehat{s}_{N}(y)-\mu_{\theta}(y)\|_{2}^{2}\Big]+\|\mu_{\theta}(y)-s_{m}(y)\|_{2}^{2} + 2 𝔼 X ( i ) ∼ iid P θ ( ⋅ ∣ y ) [ ( s ^ N ( y ) − μ θ ( y ) ) ⊤ ( μ θ ( y ) − s m ( y ) ) ] \displaystyle\qquad\quad+2{\mathbb{E}}_{X^{(i)}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}P_{\theta}(\cdot\mid y)}\!\Big[(\widehat{s}_{N}(y)-\mu_{\theta}(y))^{\top}(\mu_{\theta}(y)-s_{m}(y))\Big] = tr Cov X ( i ) ∼ iid P θ ( ⋅ ∣ y ) ( s ^ N ( y ) ) + ∥ μ θ ( y ) − s m ( y ) ∥ 2 2 \displaystyle\quad=\operatorname{tr}\operatorname{Cov}_{X^{(i)}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}P_{\theta}(\cdot\mid y)}(\widehat{s}_{N}(y))+\|\mu_{\theta}(y)-s_{m}(y)\|_{2}^{2} + 2 ​ ( 𝔼 X ( i ) ∼ iid P θ ( ⋅ ∣ y ) [ s ^ N ( y ) ] − μ θ ( y ) ) ⊤ ⏟ = μ θ ​ ( y ) − μ θ ​ ( y ) = 0 ​ ( μ θ ​ ( y ) − s m ​ ( y ) ) \displaystyle\qquad\quad+2\underbrace{\left({\mathbb{E}}_{X^{(i)}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}P_{\theta}(\cdot\mid y)}[\widehat{s}_{N}(y)]-\mu_{\theta}(y)\right)^{\top}}_{=\mu_{\theta}(y)-\mu_{\theta}(y)=0}(\mu_{\theta}(y)-s_{m}(y)) = ∥ μ θ ( y ) − s m ( y ) ∥ 2 2 + 1 N tr Cov X ∼ P θ ( ⋅ ∣ y ) ( h η ( y , X ) ) . \displaystyle\quad=\|\mu_{\theta}(y)-s_{m}(y)\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{N}\operatorname{tr}\operatorname{Cov}_{X\sim P_{\theta}(\cdot\mid y)}(h_{\eta}(y,X)). ∎ The first term is the squared bias from the learned posterior and does not depend on N N .
The second term is posterior Monte Carlo variance and decays as 1 / N 1/N .
It requires β > 1 − d / 2 \beta>1-d/2 : ‖ h η ​ ( y , x ) ‖ 2 2 \|h_{\eta}(y,x)\|_{2}^{2} scales as ‖ y − x ‖ 2 2 ​ ( β − 1 ) \|y-x\|_{2}^{2(\beta-1)} near x = y x=y , hence the covariance can be infinite in 1 dimension. A.4.2 Empirical Score Comparisons Estimators and evaluation. As a reminder, we compare three approaches: • the posterior route uses the identity directly in conjunction with samples from the denoising posterior, averaging the known clean-conditioned score h η ​ ( y , X ) h_{\eta}(y,X) (Eq. 40 ) over 192 192 draws. • ET-DSM learns a neural network s θ ​ ( y , η ) s_{\theta}(y,\eta) by regressing against h η ​ ( Y , X ) h_{\eta}(Y,X) using clean–noisy pairs. • Hyvärinen score matching uses a similar neural architecture
but trains on noisy data alone. While in principle it does not require the noise parameters as an input (cf. Sec. 5.2 ), we condition the network on the noise parameters to reuse it across experiments (i.e., not have to retrain it in each setting). Noise magnitude. All comparisons in this appendix use the five-parameter generalized Gaussian noise of Sec. 5.1 , η = ( β , λ , σ , u , v ) \eta=(\beta,\lambda,\sigma,u,v) with Σ = σ 2 ​ S ​ ( u , v ) \Sigma=\sigma^{2}S(u,v) , on the two-dimensional Eight-Gaussians data.
The scale σ \sigma alone does not determine how far the noise displaces an observation, since the displacement also
depends on β \beta and λ \lambda . To compare parameter combinations at equal noise strength, we use the per-coordinate root mean
square (RMS) displacement of a noise draw r η r_{\eta} : c β , d 2 = β 2 / β ​ Γ ​ ( ( d + 2 ) / β ) d ​ Γ ​ ( d / β ) , ρ = c β , d σ , r η = ρ λ − 1 / β , c_{\beta,d}^{2}=\frac{\beta^{2/\beta}\,\Gamma((d+2)/\beta)}{d\,\Gamma(d/\beta)},\qquad\rho=c_{\beta,d}\,\sigma,\qquad r_{\eta}=\rho\,\lambda^{-1/\beta}, (42) where d d is the ambient dimension, here d = 2 d=2 .
We call ρ \rho , the value of r η r_{\eta} at λ = 1 \lambda=1 , the reference RMS; it is the magnitude coordinate on which the five-parameter models are conditioned (App. A.8 ). In the experimental appendices and figures, ρ \rho denotes this scalar, unrelated to the kernel discrepancy of Sec. 4 , as should be apparent from context. Noise-parameter combinations and ground truth. The sweeps of Table 1 vary one parameter at a time around the default combination ( β , λ , σ , u , v ) = ( 1.4 , 1.8 , 0.45 , 0 , 0.6 ) (\beta,\lambda,\sigma,u,v)=(1.4,\,1.8,\,0.45,\,0,\,0.6) : β ∈ { 1.3 , 1.5 , 1.75 , 2 } \beta\in\{1.3,1.5,1.75,2\} , λ ∈ { 1 , 1.4 , 1.8 , 2.5 } \lambda\in\{1,1.4,1.8,2.5\} , σ ∈ { 0.1 , 0.25 , 0.45 , 0.75 } \sigma\in\{0.1,0.25,0.45,0.75\} , and u , v ∈ { − 0.6 , − 0.3 , 0 , 0.3 , 0.6 } u,v\in\{-0.6,-0.3,0,0.3,0.6\} , where the other coordinate is held at 0 0 while u u or v v is swept.
All combinations are specified through the reference RMS ρ \rho and converted to σ \sigma at the combination’s β \beta ; the β \beta sweep therefore holds ρ = 0.570 \rho=0.570 fixed while the tail shape changes, and the magnitude sweep ρ ∈ { 0.13 , 0.32 , 0.57 , 0.95 } \rho\in\{0.13,0.32,0.57,0.95\} is reported as σ ∈ { 0.1 , 0.25 , 0.45 , 0.75 } \sigma\in\{0.1,0.25,0.45,0.75\} at β = 1.4 \beta=1.4 .
In total there are 22 22 parameter combinations with 192 192 noisy observations each. When the noise is Gaussian, the noised score is analytic. This is not the case for generalized Gaussian noise, so to obtain a high-quality reference for evaluation we construct a numerical ground truth by importance sampling from the clean data distribution:
a bank of 160,000 160{,}000 clean particles is reweighted by the generalized Gaussian likelihood at the observation, and the ground-truth score is the weighted average of the clean-conditioned score h η h_{\eta} (Eq. 40 ) over the bank.

The bank is split into two independent halves. The ground truth is the average of the two half-bank estimates, and the NMSE between the halves, reported as the ground-truth disagreement in Table 1 , measures the precision of the ground truth itself.
The posterior route averages 192 192 posterior draws per observation. The direct estimators are evaluated at the same parameter combinations with the same number of observations and the same bank
size, but with separately generated observations and ground truths, so Table 6 lists the ground-truth precision of each evaluation. Accuracy across the sweeps. Figure 3 shows NMSE and cosine similarity at every parameter combination, which is the data behind the means presented in Table 1 in the main text.
ET-DSM has the lowest NMSE in every sweep, followed by Hyvärinen score matching and then the posterior route. The three estimators agree in direction and magnitude: the sweep-mean cosine similarity exceeds 0.95 0.95 for every estimator, and the norm ratios lie within a few percent of one (Table 4 ).
The largest error of the posterior route occurs at the smallest scale σ = 0.1 \sigma=0.1 , where the importance-sampled ground truth is also least precise (Table 6 ): as the noise shrinks, the posterior concentrates on few bank particles, the effective sample size drops, and the two half-bank estimates disagree by an NMSE far larger than at the other combinations. As noted in the main text, while the two direct estimators were ensured to be well-trained in order for a maximally rigorous comparison (see Training of the direct estimators below), no such procedure was done for the posterior, so its slightly lower accuracy might be due to undertraining. We can also compare the posterior model draws and the importance posterior directly. Table 5 reports the error of the
posterior mean, the ratio of the posterior covariance traces, the total variation distance between the assignments of posterior mass to the nearest mixture centers, and the energy distance between the two posteriors, together with its excess over the energy distance between two independent resamples of the importance posterior. The results show that the learned posterior is centered and assigns modes correctly; its remaining error is a mild overdispersion (trace ratio 1.04 1.04 – 1.11 1.11 ), consistent with the nonzero score NMSE that the posterior route retains as N → ∞ N\to\infty (Table 9 ) and pointing to mild undertraining. The sweeps above span less than one decade of noise magnitude.
Figure 4 supplements this by evaluating the posterior
route over three decades, using the isotropic Gaussian and generalized Gaussian noise with the remaining parameters fixed. Cosine similarity dips at intermediate magnitudes, with the dip confined to the observations whose ground-truth score has below-median norm: where the ground-truth score is small, a small absolute error produces a large angular error, and the score NMSE in this region stays at or below 0.010 0.010 . Replacing the learned posterior by the exact one (analytic for Gaussian noise, importance-sampled for generalized Gaussian noise) reproduces the dip (Table 7 ), which places the responsibility in the Monte Carlo estimate at those observations rather than in the learned model. At the smallest magnitudes, shaded gray, the half-bank ground-truth NMSE exceeds 0.01 0.01 and the comparison is thus limited by the ground truth. With the factor 1 2 \tfrac{1}{2} included by convention, the objectives at a fixed parameter combination η \eta are D η ​ ( θ ) \displaystyle D_{\eta}(\theta) = 1 2 ​ ℒ ET ​ - ​ DSM ​ ( θ , η ) , \displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{ET\text{-}DSM}}(\theta;\eta), H η ​ ( θ ) \displaystyle H_{\eta}(\theta) = 𝔼 Y ∼ m η ​ [ 1 2 ​ ‖ s θ ​ ( Y , η ) ‖ 2 2 + div Y ⁡ s θ ​ ( Y , η ) ] , \displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{Y\sim m_{\eta}}[\tfrac{1}{2}\|s_{\theta}(Y,\eta)\|_{2}^{2}+\operatorname{div}_{Y}s_{\theta}(Y,\eta)], (43) with ℒ ET ​ - ​ DSM \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{ET\text{-}DSM}} from Eq. 25 and m η = p ∗ q η m_{\eta}=p*q_{\eta} the noisy marginal. Each minibatch draws one parameter combination from the law used to train the five-parameter Eight-Gaussians posterior (App. A.8 ). The objectives are averaged over combinations with the weight r η 2 r_{\eta}^{2} in order for the small-noise cases not to dominate the loss. Both objectives have known population minima, so we can ensure that the training has converged.
Let s η = ∇ log ⁡ m η s_{\eta}=\nabla\log m_{\eta} be the noisy score, I η = 𝔼 Y ∼ m η ​ [ ‖ s η ​ ( Y ) ‖ 2 2 ] I_{\eta}={\mathbb{E}}_{Y\sim m_{\eta}}[\|s_{\eta}(Y)\|_{2}^{2}] its expected squared norm, and C η = 𝔼 X ∼ p , Y ∼ q η ( ⋅ ∣ X ) [ ∥ h η ( Y , X ) ∥ 2 2 ] C_{\eta}={\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim p,\,Y\sim q_{\eta}(\cdot\mid X)}[\|h_{\eta}(Y,X)\|_{2}^{2}] that of the clean-conditioned score (Eq. 40 ).
Under the integration-by-parts conditions of score matching, we get [ 15 ; 45 ] : H η ∗ \displaystyle H_{\eta}^{*} = − I η / 2 , D η ∗ = ( C η − I η ) / 2 , \displaystyle=-I_{\eta}/2,\qquad D_{\eta}^{*}=(C_{\eta}-I_{\eta})/2, (44) H η ​ ( θ ) − H η ∗ \displaystyle H_{\eta}(\theta)-H_{\eta}^{*} = D η ​ ( θ ) − D η ∗ = 1 2 ​ 𝔼 Y ∼ m η ​ [ ‖ s θ ​ ( Y , η ) − s η ​ ( Y ) ‖ 2 2 ] , \displaystyle=D_{\eta}(\theta)-D_{\eta}^{*}=\tfrac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}}_{Y\sim m_{\eta}}[\|s_{\theta}(Y,\eta)-s_{\eta}(Y)\|_{2}^{2}], so both objectives exceed their minima by half the mean squared score error, and this excess divided by I η / 2 I_{\eta}/2 is the score NMSE at that combination.
For the two-dimensional generalized Gaussian, C η = λ 2 ​ tr ⁡ ( S − 1 ) 2 ​ σ 2 ​ ( β λ ) 2 − 2 / β ​ 1 Γ ⁡ ( 2 / β ) , C_{\eta}=\frac{\lambda^{2}\operatorname{tr}(S^{-1})}{2\sigma^{2}}\left(\frac{\beta}{\lambda}\right)^{2-2/\beta}\frac{1}{\Gamma(2/\beta)}, while I η I_{\eta} is estimated on the validation set from the inner product of the two half-bank ground-truth
scores.
Validation is evaluated every 2,000 2{,}000 updates.
We can thus evaluate the theoretical minima from Eq. 44 and perform the same weighted average as for the loss to obtain a loss floor in Fig. 5 .
The validation curves settle near their floors, and the final relative gaps are between 1 % 1\% and 5 % 5\% (Table 8 ), so (at least at the chosen validation parameter values) the two network sets are near-optimally trained.

Compute Budgets. The three estimators share the clean distribution and the noise-parameter law above.
Each direct estimation network is trained on 327.68 327.68 million fresh clean–noisy pairs under 80,000 80{,}000 draws of the parameter combination; the
posterior model, which is also the model behind the diffusion experiments of App. A.6.1 , was
trained on 22.4 22.4 billion pairs under 560,000 560{,}000 draws of the parameter combination.
Naturally, the networks are not comparable, as the direct estimation networks estimate the score alone, while we use the same posterior network for all three presented applications.
For the posterior route, Eq. 41 separates the error at an observation into the
squared bias of the learned posterior, which additional draws do not reduce, and the Monte Carlo variance, which
decays as 1 / N 1/N .

fixing r r away from one controls variance amplification. A.5 Appendix for Parameter Estimation A.5.1 Theoretical Appendix This subsection collects the formal results supporting the estimation criterion in
Sec. 5.2 , specialized to the generalized Gaussian case. For reference, the population criterion from Eq. 28 can be
written as J 0 ​ ( β , λ , Σ ) = 𝔼 Y ∼ m ​ [ ‖ s m ​ ( Y ) − λ ​ G ​ ( Y , β , Σ ) ‖ 2 2 ] , J_{0}(\beta,\lambda,\Sigma)={\mathbb{E}}_{Y\sim m}\left[\left\|s_{m}(Y)-\lambda G(Y;\beta,\Sigma)\right\|_{2}^{2}\right], (49) where G ( Y ; β , Σ ) = − 1 β ∇ Y PD ES Σ − 1 , β ( P ( ⋅ ∣ Y ) , Y ) G(Y;\beta,\Sigma)=-\frac{1}{\beta}\nabla_{Y}^{\mathrm{PD}}\mathrm{ES}_{\Sigma^{-1},\beta}\big(P(\cdot\mid Y),Y\big) is the residual field previously defined in Eq. 29 . Throughout this subsection, we adopt the regularity condition discussed in App. A.2.3 : 𝔼 ⁡ [ ‖ y − X ‖ Σ − 1 β − 1 ∣ Y = y ] < ∞ {\mathbb{E}}\!\left[\|y-X\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta-1}\mid Y=y\right]<\infty .
As discussed there, this condition is satisfied whenever the data density is locally bounded near the observation y y . The profiled scalar scale is the least-squares coefficient in Eq. 30 ; when a finite valid set Λ = [ λ min , λ max ] \Lambda=[\lambda_{\min},\lambda_{\max}] is imposed, the implementation uses Π Λ ​ ( a ) = min ⁡ { λ max , max ⁡ { λ min , a } } \Pi_{\Lambda}(a)=\min\{\lambda_{\max},\max\{\lambda_{\min},a\}\} . Theorem A.1 is the population statement: without a trace constraint, the true parameters are
identified only up to the standard elliptical scale equivalence; with Tr ⁡ ( Σ ) = d \Tr(\Sigma)=d , the normalized representative
is unique. Proposition A.2 turns this population identification into consistency of the
plug-in noise-parameter estimator when the auxiliary score estimator and Monte Carlo posterior field are consistent.
Corollary A.3 justifies profiling λ \lambda , which is the reduction used by
Alg. 1 . Theorem A.1 (Oracle Identifiability of the Noise Parameters) Let Θ = Δ { ( β ∈ ( 0 , 2 ] , λ > 0 , Σ ≻ 0 ) } \Theta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\{(\beta\in(0,2],\lambda>0,\Sigma\succ 0)\} and let the true parameters of the
noising distribution be denoted by θ ∗ = ( β ∗ , λ ∗ , Σ ∗ ) \theta^{*}=(\beta^{*},\lambda^{*},\Sigma^{*}) . 1) The optimizers under the true posterior P ( ⋅ ∣ Y ) P(\cdot\mid Y) of the population-level objective J 0 J_{0} in Eq. 49 include the equivalence class { ( β ∗ , c β ∗ / 2 ​ λ ∗ , c ​ Σ ∗ ) ∣ c ∈ ℝ + } . \{(\beta^{*},c^{\beta^{*}/2}\,\lambda^{*},c\,\Sigma^{*})\mid c\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\}. In particular, J 0 ​ ( β ∗ , c β ∗ / 2 ​ λ ∗ , c ​ Σ ∗ ) = J 0 ​ ( β ∗ , λ ∗ , Σ ∗ ) ≡ 0 , ∀ c ∈ ℝ + . J_{0}(\beta^{*},c^{\beta^{*}/2}\,\lambda^{*},c\,\Sigma^{*})=J_{0}(\beta^{*},\lambda^{*},\Sigma^{*})\equiv 0,~\forall c\in\mathbb{R}^{+}. 2) Assume the true equivalence class is represented by the normalized tuple θ ∗ = ( β ∗ , λ ∗ , Σ ∗ ) \theta^{*}=(\beta^{*},\lambda^{*},\Sigma^{*}) with Tr ⁡ ( Σ ∗ ) = d , \Tr(\Sigma^{*})=d, that is Θ d = { ( β , λ , Σ ) : β ∈ ( 0 , 2 ] , λ > 0 , Σ ≻ 0 , Tr ( Σ ) = d } \Theta_{d}=\{(\beta,\lambda,\Sigma):\beta\in(0,2],\lambda>0,\Sigma\succ 0,\operatorname{Tr}(\Sigma)=d\} . Then, the restricted population optimization problem with Tr ⁡ ( Σ ) = d \Tr(\Sigma)=d admits a unique restricted optimizer,
namely the representative parameter tuple ( β ∗ , λ ∗ , Σ ∗ ) (\beta^{*},\lambda^{*},\Sigma^{*}) . The proof is given in App. A.5.3 . It first records the scale orbit of equivalent
generalized-Gaussian parameterizations and then uses the trace constraint plus convolution identifiability to pin
down the normalized representative. Proposition A.2 (Consistency of the Plug-in Estimator) Let θ = ( β , λ , Σ ) \theta=(\beta,\lambda,\Sigma) denote the noise parameters and let Θ ⊂ Θ d \Theta\subset\Theta_{d} be a compact
subset of the restricted parameter space containing the true parameter tuple θ ∗ = ( β ∗ , λ ∗ , Σ ∗ ) \theta^{*}=(\beta^{*},\lambda^{*},\Sigma^{*}) .
Assume that the posterior term in the criterion is correctly specified, i.e., it uses P ( ⋅ ∣ Y = y ) P(\cdot\mid Y=y) , and
use G β , Σ ​ ( y ) G_{\beta,\Sigma}(y) defined in Eq. 29 .
For Y 1 , … , Y n ​ ∼ iid ​ m Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}m , let J n ​ ( θ ) = Δ 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n ‖ s m ​ ( Y i ) − λ ​ G β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) ‖ 2 2 , and ​ J ^ n ​ ( θ ) = Δ 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n ‖ s ^ m , n ​ ( Y i ) − λ ​ G β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) ‖ 2 2 J_{n}(\theta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|s_{m}(Y_{i})-\lambda G_{\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}\quad\text{, and}~\widehat{J}_{n}(\theta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\widehat{s}_{m,n}(Y_{i})-\lambda G_{\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2} be the sample-based versions of the population criterion J 0 J_{0} in Eq. 49 based on the
oracle and the plug-in score estimator s ^ m , n \widehat{s}_{m,n} , respectively.
Assume J 0 J_{0} is continuous on Θ \Theta , and that J n J_{n} is uniformly consistent: sup θ ∈ Θ | J n ​ ( θ ) − J 0 ​ ( θ ) | → P 0 . \sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\left|J_{n}(\theta)-J_{0}(\theta)\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0. For the score terms, assume that s ^ m , n \widehat{s}_{m,n} is empirically L 2 L_{2} -consistent on the evaluation sample: 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n ‖ s ^ m , n ​ ( Y i ) − s m ​ ( Y i ) ‖ 2 2 = o p ​ ( 1 ) , \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\widehat{s}_{m,n}(Y_{i})-s_{m}(Y_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}=o_{p}(1), and the oracle residual w.r.t. the Energy-Tweedie term is empirically bounded in the second moment: 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n sup θ ∈ Θ ‖ s m ​ ( Y i ) − λ ​ G β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) ‖ 2 2 = O p ​ ( 1 ) . \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\left\|s_{m}(Y_{i})-\lambda G_{\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}=O_{p}(1). By Theorem A.1 , θ ∗ \theta^{*} is the unique minimizer of J 0 J_{0} on Θ \Theta ,
thus if θ ^ n \widehat{\theta}_{n} is any approximate minimizer satisfying J ^ n ​ ( θ ^ n ) ≤ inf θ ∈ Θ J ^ n ​ ( θ ) + o p ​ ( 1 ) , \widehat{J}_{n}(\widehat{\theta}_{n})\leq\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\widehat{J}_{n}(\theta)+o_{p}(1), then θ ^ n → P θ ∗ . \widehat{\theta}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}\theta^{*}. Now suppose that G β , Σ G_{\beta,\Sigma} is replaced by a Monte Carlo estimate G ^ K n , β , Σ \widehat{G}_{K_{n},\beta,\Sigma} computed from K n K_{n} posterior samples per evaluation point. If 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n sup θ ∈ Θ λ 2 ​ ‖ G ^ K n , β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) − G β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) ‖ 2 2 = o p ​ ( 1 ) , \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\lambda^{2}\left\|\widehat{G}_{K_{n},\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})-G_{\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}=o_{p}(1), then with the corresponding Monte Carlo criterion: J ^ n , K n MC ​ ( β , λ , Σ ) = Δ 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n ‖ s ^ m , n ​ ( Y i ) − λ ​ G ^ K n , β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) ‖ 2 2 , \widehat{J}_{n,K_{n}}^{\mathrm{MC}}(\beta,\lambda,\Sigma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\widehat{s}_{m,n}(Y_{i})-\lambda\widehat{G}_{K_{n},\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}, the same conclusion holds for its approximate minimizers: θ ^ n , K n MC → P θ ∗ . \widehat{\theta}^{\mathrm{MC}}_{n,K_{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}\theta^{*}. The proof is given in App. A.5.3 . Corollary A.3 ( λ \lambda Profile Closed-Form) With G ⁡ ( y , β , Σ ) G(y;\beta,\Sigma) defined in Eq. 29 , for every fixed β \beta and Σ \Sigma with 𝔼 Y ∼ m ​ [ ‖ G ⁡ ( Y , β , Σ ) ‖ 2 2 ] > 0 {\mathbb{E}}_{Y\sim m}[\|G(Y;\beta,\Sigma)\|_{2}^{2}]>0 , the unconstrained optimizer for λ \lambda in
Eq. 49 is given by Eq. 30 . If this value is positive, it is also the
optimizer under the constraint λ > 0 \lambda>0 ; otherwise the constrained infimum is approached as λ ↓ 0 \lambda\downarrow 0 . For a finite valid interval Λ = [ λ min , λ max ] \Lambda=[\lambda_{\min},\lambda_{\max}] , the constrained
optimizer is the projected coefficient Π Λ ​ [ λ ∗ ​ ( β , Σ ) ] \Pi_{\Lambda}[\lambda^{*}(\beta,\Sigma)] .
Furthermore, in the Gaussian case this simplifies to: λ ∗ ​ ( β = 2 , Σ ) = 𝔼 Y ∼ m ​ [ ⟨ s m ​ ( Y ) , Σ − 1 ​ ( 𝔼 ⁡ [ X ∣ Y ] − Y ) ⟩ ] 𝔼 Y ∼ m ​ [ ‖ Σ − 1 ​ ( 𝔼 ⁡ [ X ∣ Y ] − Y ) ‖ 2 2 ] , \lambda^{*}(\beta=2,\Sigma)=\frac{{\mathbb{E}}_{Y\sim m}\left[\langle s_{m}(Y),\Sigma^{-1}({\mathbb{E}}[X\mid Y]-Y)\rangle\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{Y\sim m}\left[\|\Sigma^{-1}({\mathbb{E}}[X\mid Y]-Y)\|^{2}_{2}\right]}, (50) and in the isotropic case Σ = σ 2 ​ I d \Sigma=\sigma^{2}I_{d} this further simplifies to: λ ∗ ​ ( β = 2 , σ 2 ​ I d ) = σ 2 ​ 𝔼 Y ∼ m ​ [ ⟨ s m ​ ( Y ) , 𝔼 ⁡ [ X ∣ Y ] − Y ⟩ ] 𝔼 Y ∼ m ​ [ ‖ 𝔼 ⁡ [ X ∣ Y ] − Y ‖ 2 2 ] . \lambda^{*}(\beta=2,\sigma^{2}I_{d})=\sigma^{2}\,\frac{{\mathbb{E}}_{Y\sim m}\left[\langle s_{m}(Y),{\mathbb{E}}[X\mid Y]-Y\rangle\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{Y\sim m}\left[\|{\mathbb{E}}[X\mid Y]-Y\|^{2}_{2}\right]}. (51) In words, this is the global least-squares projection coefficient of the noisy score onto the residual field
induced by the fixed ( β , Σ ) (\beta,\Sigma) . The proof is given in App. A.5.3 . The algorithm below combines these ingredients in the form used in the estimation experiment: Σ \Sigma is
parameterized with trace normalization, λ \lambda is profiled by Cor. A.3 and projected to the
valid interval Λ \Lambda , and the remaining shape parameters are updated by gradient steps on the profiled
objective. A.5.2 Extra Empirical Results We present the full results of the experiment in Tab. 2 in Tab. 10 . To recap:
the correctly specified family estimates ( β , λ , u , v ) (\beta,\lambda,u,v) ;
the Gaussian control fixes β = 2 \beta=2 while retaining the full learned SPD matrix; the
isotropic generalized control fixes u = v = 0 u=v=0 while estimating ( β , λ ) (\beta,\lambda) ; and the isotropic Gaussian control imposes both restrictions. Every family is crossed with the four score/posterior source combinations on the same paired estimation and held-out observations. The truth is ( β ∗ , λ ∗ , u ∗ , v ∗ ) = ( 1.4 , 1.8 , 0.25 , 0.35 ) (\beta^{*},\lambda^{*},u^{*},v^{*})=(1.4,1.8,0.25,0.35) , and all held-out errors use the same oracle noisy score.
As in Table 2 , the “oracle” score and posterior are the importance-sampled ground truth, and the “learned” score and posterior
are the Hyvärinen estimator and the SPD-conditioned Engressor.
The values are estimated from 384 384 estimation and 384 384 held-out observations per noise level, pooled over the three noise levels (totaling 1152 1152 observations each), with 256 256 posterior samples per observation.
We generate 5 replicates of the data, each paired with its own trained score estimator (in the learned-score case). For the learned posterior entries, each data replicate is additionally fitted with each of 3 3 separately trained posterior models.
Score estimates and posterior draws are generated once per observation before the fit and held fixed during the optimization, so the plug-in objective is deterministic given the data replicate and the same samples serve every fitted family. which is moreover θ ~ = θ ∗ \tilde{\theta}=\theta^{*} . Reminder: for θ = ( β , λ , Σ ) \theta=(\beta,\lambda,\Sigma) , Ψ θ ​ ( u ) = Δ λ ​ Σ − 1 ​ ‖ u ‖ Σ − 1 β − 2 ​ u . \Psi_{\theta}(u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\lambda\Sigma^{-1}\|u\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta-2}u. The scalar radial factors are positive, so the vector parts Σ ~ − 1 ​ u \tilde{\Sigma}^{-1}u and ( Σ ∗ ) − 1 ​ u (\Sigma^{*})^{-1}u must be collinear.
Pre-multiplying by Σ ∗ \Sigma^{*} and writing B = Δ Σ ∗ ​ Σ ~ − 1 B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\Sigma^{*}\tilde{\Sigma}^{-1} gives λ ~ ​ ‖ u ‖ Σ ~ − 1 β ~ − 2 ​ B ​ u = λ ∗ ​ ‖ u ‖ ( Σ ∗ ) − 1 β ∗ − 2 ​ u . \tilde{\lambda}\|u\|_{\tilde{\Sigma}^{-1}}^{\tilde{\beta}-2}Bu=\lambda^{*}\|u\|_{(\Sigma^{*})^{-1}}^{\beta^{*}-2}u. We also have B ​ e i = a i ​ e i Be_{i}=a_{i}e_{i} for a basis { e i } \{e_{i}\} and constants a i a_{i} , and that B ⁡ ( e i + e j ) B(e_{i}+e_{j}) must be parallel to e i + e j e_{i}+e_{j} . Thus a i = a j a_{i}=a_{j} for all i , j i,j , and hence B = b ​ I B=bI for some b > 0 b>0 .
Equivalently, for c = Δ b − 1 > 0 c\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}b^{-1}>0 , Σ ~ = c ​ Σ ∗ . \tilde{\Sigma}=c\Sigma^{*}. Substituting Σ ~ = c ​ Σ ∗ \tilde{\Sigma}=c\Sigma^{*} into the full identity above gives λ ~ c − β ~ / 2 ∥ u ∥ ( Σ ∗ ) − 1 β ~ − 2 ( Σ ∗ ) − 1 u = λ ∗ ∥ u ∥ ( Σ ∗ ) − 1 β ∗ − 2 ( Σ ∗ ) − 1 u . \tilde{\lambda}c^{-\tilde{\beta}/2}\|u\|_{(\Sigma^{*})^{-1}}^{\tilde{\beta}-2}(\Sigma^{*})^{-1}u=\lambda^{*}\|u\|_{(\Sigma^{*})^{-1}}^{\beta^{*}-2}(\Sigma^{*})^{-1}u. Since Σ ∗ \Sigma^{*} is positive definite and u ≠ 0 u\neq 0 , we can cancel ( Σ ∗ ) − 1 ​ u (\Sigma^{*})^{-1}u . Defining the Mahalanobis
radius r = Δ u ⊤ ​ ( Σ ∗ ) − 1 ​ u , r\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\sqrt{u^{\top}(\Sigma^{*})^{-1}u}, we obtain the scalar relation λ ~ c − β ~ / 2 r β ~ − 2 = λ ∗ r β ∗ − 2 , for all r > 0 . \tilde{\lambda}c^{-\tilde{\beta}/2}r^{\tilde{\beta}-2}=\lambda^{*}r^{\beta^{*}-2},\quad\text{for all }r>0. This identity can hold for all r > 0 r>0 only if the powers of r r agree: β ~ − 2 = β ∗ − 2 , \tilde{\beta}-2=\beta^{*}-2, hence β ~ = β ∗ \tilde{\beta}=\beta^{*} . Plugging back in gives λ ~ c − β ∗ / 2 = λ ∗ , \tilde{\lambda}c^{-\beta^{*}/2}=\lambda^{*}, or equivalently λ ~ = c β ∗ / 2 ​ λ ∗ \tilde{\lambda}=c^{\beta^{*}/2}\lambda^{*} . Therefore, θ ~ = ( β ∗ , c β ∗ / 2 ​ λ ∗ , c ​ Σ ∗ ) , \tilde{\theta}=(\beta^{*},c^{\beta^{*}/2}\lambda^{*},c\Sigma^{*}), so the restricted optimizer θ ~ \tilde{\theta} lies in the same optimal orbit as θ ∗ \theta^{*} . Finally, since Tr ⁡ ( Σ ~ ) = d \Tr(\tilde{\Sigma})=d and Tr ⁡ ( Σ ∗ ) = d \Tr(\Sigma^{*})=d , we have c ​ d = d cd=d , hence c = 1 c=1 and θ ~ = θ ∗ \tilde{\theta}=\theta^{*} .
Thus the normalized true tuple is the unique restricted optimizer. Thus, restricting the scale of the “covariance”
uniquely pins down the minimizer as it pins down the constant c c above. This also proves that the above minimizer
equivalence class is the only one for Eq. 49 , otherwise we’d not be able to obtain the unique minimizer (i.e., by contradiction). Furthermore, this restricted unique minimizer coincides with
the true parameter tuple or the normalized representative. ∎ Proof of Proposition A.2 Reminder: following Corollary A.3 , we have the following shorthand: G β , Σ ( y ) = Δ − 1 β ∇ y PD ES Σ − 1 , β ( P ( ⋅ ∣ Y = y ) , y ) . G_{\beta,\Sigma}(y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}-\frac{1}{\beta}\nabla_{y}^{\mathrm{PD}}\mathrm{ES}_{\Sigma^{-1},\beta}\bigl(P(\cdot\mid Y=y),y\bigr). The population criterion J 0 J_{0} can then be written as: J 0 ​ ( θ ) = 𝔼 Y ∼ m ​ [ ‖ s m ​ ( Y ) − λ ​ G β , Σ ​ ( Y ) ‖ 2 2 ] . J_{0}(\theta)={\mathbb{E}}_{Y\sim m}\left[\left\|s_{m}(Y)-\lambda G_{\beta,\Sigma}(Y)\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]. Using this we can define: • the oracle empirical criterion: J n ​ ( β , λ , Σ ) = Δ 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n ‖ s m ​ ( Y i ) − λ ​ G β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) ‖ 2 2 , J_{n}(\beta,\lambda,\Sigma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|s_{m}(Y_{i})-\lambda G_{\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}, • the plug-in empirical criterion: J ^ n ​ ( β , λ , Σ ) = Δ 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n ‖ s ^ m , n ​ ( Y i ) − λ ​ G β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) ‖ 2 2 . \widehat{J}_{n}(\beta,\lambda,\Sigma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\widehat{s}_{m,n}(Y_{i})-\lambda G_{\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}. • the Monte Carlo plug-in empirical criterion , using K n K_{n} Monte Carlo samples to estimate G β , Σ G_{\beta,\Sigma} : J ^ n , K n MC ​ ( β , λ , Σ ) = Δ 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n ‖ s ^ m , n ​ ( Y i ) − λ ​ G ^ K n , β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) ‖ 2 2 . \widehat{J}_{n,K_{n}}^{\mathrm{MC}}(\beta,\lambda,\Sigma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\widehat{s}_{m,n}(Y_{i})-\lambda\widehat{G}_{K_{n},\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}. For θ = ( β , λ , Σ ) \theta=(\beta,\lambda,\Sigma) , write G θ ​ ( y ) = Δ G β , Σ ​ ( y ) G_{\theta}(y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}G_{\beta,\Sigma}(y) and let e i = Δ s ^ m , n ​ ( Y i ) − s m ​ ( Y i ) , r i ​ ( θ ) = Δ s m ​ ( Y i ) − λ ​ G θ ​ ( Y i ) . e_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\widehat{s}_{m,n}(Y_{i})-s_{m}(Y_{i}),\qquad r_{i}(\theta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}s_{m}(Y_{i})-\lambda G_{\theta}(Y_{i}). We wish to see what happens to the plug-in empirical criterion when a consistent plug-in estimator is used.
We have: s ^ m , n ​ ( Y i ) − λ ​ G β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) = s ^ m , n ​ ( Y i ) − s m ​ ( Y i ) + s m ​ ( Y i ) − λ ​ G β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) = e i + r i ​ ( θ ) . \widehat{s}_{m,n}(Y_{i})-\lambda G_{\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})=\widehat{s}_{m,n}(Y_{i})-s_{m}(Y_{i})+s_{m}(Y_{i})-\lambda G_{\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})=e_{i}+r_{i}(\theta). Therefore, for each θ = ( β , λ , Σ ) ∈ Θ \theta=(\beta,\lambda,\Sigma)\in\Theta , J ^ n ​ ( θ ) − J n ​ ( θ ) \displaystyle\widehat{J}_{n}(\theta)-J_{n}(\theta) = 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n [ ‖ s ^ m , n ​ ( Y i ) − λ ​ G β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) ‖ 2 2 − ‖ s m ​ ( Y i ) − λ ​ G β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) ‖ 2 2 ] \displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\left\|\widehat{s}_{m,n}(Y_{i})-\lambda G_{\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}-\left\|s_{m}(Y_{i})-\lambda G_{\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}\right] = 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n [ ‖ r i ​ ( θ ) + e i ‖ 2 2 − ‖ r i ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 ] \displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\|r_{i}(\theta)+e_{i}\|_{2}^{2}-\|r_{i}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}\right] = 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n ( 2 ​ ⟨ e i , r i ​ ( θ ) ⟩ + ‖ e i ‖ 2 2 ) . \displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(2\langle e_{i},r_{i}(\theta)\rangle+\|e_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\right). The proposition assumes directly that sup θ ∈ Θ | J n ​ ( θ ) − J 0 ​ ( θ ) | → P 0 . \sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\left|J_{n}(\theta)-J_{0}(\theta)\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}0. It remains to bound the plug-in error J ^ n − J n \widehat{J}_{n}-J_{n} .
By the triangle and Cauchy–Schwarz inequalities: sup θ ∈ Θ | J ^ n ​ ( θ ) − J n ​ ( θ ) | \displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\left|\widehat{J}_{n}(\theta)-J_{n}(\theta)\right| = sup θ ∈ Θ | 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n ( 2 ​ ⟨ e i , r i ​ ( θ ) ⟩ + ‖ e i ‖ 2 2 ) | \displaystyle=\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(2\langle e_{i},r_{i}(\theta)\rangle+\|e_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\right)\right| ≤ sup θ ∈ Θ 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n ( 2 ​ | ⟨ e i , r i ​ ( θ ) ⟩ | + ‖ e i ‖ 2 2 ) \displaystyle\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(2\left|\langle e_{i},r_{i}(\theta)\rangle\right|+\|e_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\right) ≤ 2 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n ‖ e i ‖ 2 ​ sup θ ∈ Θ ‖ r i ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 + 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n ‖ e i ‖ 2 2 . \displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|e_{i}\|_{2}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|r_{i}(\theta)\|_{2}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|e_{i}\|_{2}^{2}. We have assumed that the plug-in estimator has an empirical L 2 L_{2} -consistency on the evaluation sample: 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n ‖ e i ‖ 2 2 = 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n ‖ s ^ m , n ​ ( Y i ) − s m ​ ( Y i ) ‖ 2 2 = o p ​ ( 1 ) . \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|e_{i}\right\|^{2}_{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\widehat{s}_{m,n}(Y_{i})-s_{m}(Y_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}=o_{p}(1). This is an empirical analogue of Fisher-divergence consistency, the latter being common for score-matching
estimators [ 38 ] .
We leave it as a high-level assumption since its verification depends on the exact score estimator, model class,
data regularity, and other factors.
This means that: sup θ ∈ Θ | J ^ n ​ ( θ ) − J n ​ ( θ ) | ≤ 2 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n ‖ e i ‖ 2 ​ sup θ ∈ Θ ‖ r i ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 + o p ​ ( 1 ) . \sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\left|\widehat{J}_{n}(\theta)-J_{n}(\theta)\right|\leq\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|e_{i}\|_{2}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|r_{i}(\theta)\|_{2}+o_{p}(1). We can further handle the remaining term by applying the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality again: 2 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n ‖ e i ‖ 2 ​ sup θ ∈ Θ ‖ r i ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 ≤ 2 ​ ( 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n ‖ e i ‖ 2 2 ) 1 / 2 ​ ( 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n sup θ ∈ Θ ‖ r i ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 ) 1 / 2 . \frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|e_{i}\|_{2}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|r_{i}(\theta)\|_{2}\leq 2\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|e_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|r_{i}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}\right)^{1/2}. So by the same assumption, the first factor is o p ​ ( 1 ) o_{p}(1) .
For the second term, we have assumed that it is empirically bounded in the second moment: 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n sup θ ∈ Θ ‖ s m ​ ( Y i ) − λ ​ G θ ​ ( Y i ) ‖ 2 2 = O p ​ ( 1 ) . \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\left\|s_{m}(Y_{i})-\lambda G_{\theta}(Y_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}=O_{p}(1). Thus, the whole is o p ​ ( 1 ) o_{p}(1) : sup θ ∈ Θ | J ^ n ​ ( θ ) − J n ​ ( θ ) | = o p ​ ( 1 ) . \sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\left|\widehat{J}_{n}(\theta)-J_{n}(\theta)\right|=o_{p}(1). By the triangle inequality, we have: sup θ ∈ Θ | J ^ n ​ ( θ ) − J 0 ​ ( θ ) | ≤ sup θ ∈ Θ | J ^ n ​ ( θ ) − J n ​ ( θ ) | + sup θ ∈ Θ | J n ​ ( θ ) − J 0 ​ ( θ ) | . \sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\left|\widehat{J}_{n}(\theta)-J_{0}(\theta)\right|\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\left|\widehat{J}_{n}(\theta)-J_{n}(\theta)\right|+\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\left|J_{n}(\theta)-J_{0}(\theta)\right|. The first term is o p ​ ( 1 ) o_{p}(1) by the above, and the second term is o p ​ ( 1 ) o_{p}(1) by assumption. Hence: sup θ ∈ Θ | J ^ n ​ ( θ ) − J 0 ​ ( θ ) | = o p ​ ( 1 ) . \sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\left|\widehat{J}_{n}(\theta)-J_{0}(\theta)\right|=o_{p}(1). We will use Theorem 5.7 in Vaart [44] with M n ​ ( θ ) = − J ^ n ​ ( θ ) M_{n}(\theta)=-\widehat{J}_{n}(\theta) and M ⁡ ( θ ) = − J 0 ​ ( θ ) M(\theta)=-J_{0}(\theta) .
First, the last result means: J ^ n ​ ( θ ^ n ) ≤ inf θ ∈ Θ J ^ n ​ ( θ ) + o p ​ ( 1 ) ≤ J ^ n ​ ( θ ∗ ) + o p ​ ( 1 ) . \widehat{J}_{n}(\widehat{\theta}_{n})\leq\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\widehat{J}_{n}(\theta)+o_{p}(1)\leq\widehat{J}_{n}(\theta^{*})+o_{p}(1). Second: let A ε = { θ ∈ Θ : d ⁡ ( θ , θ ∗ ) ≥ ε } . A_{\varepsilon}=\left\{\theta\in\Theta:d\left(\theta,\theta^{*}\right)\geq\varepsilon\right\}. As Θ \Theta is assumed compact and J 0 J_{0} is assumed continuous, J 0 J_{0} attains its minimum on A ε A_{\varepsilon} .
Theorem A.1 already gives θ ∗ \theta^{*} as the unique minimizer on the compact set Θ \Theta .
Hence: inf θ ∈ A ε J 0 ​ ( θ ) > J 0 ​ ( θ ∗ ) . \inf_{\theta\in A_{\varepsilon}}J_{0}(\theta)>J_{0}(\theta^{*}). Thus, by Theorem 5.7 in Vaart [44] , we have that θ ^ n → P θ ∗ . \widehat{\theta}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}\theta^{*}. Monte Carlo extension: We denote the Monte Carlo plug-in for G β , Σ G_{\beta,\Sigma} as: G ^ K n , β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) = Δ Σ − 1 ​ 1 K n ​ ∑ k = 1 K n ‖ X i , k − Y i ‖ Σ − 1 β − 2 ​ ( X i , k − Y i ) , \widehat{G}_{K_{n},\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\Sigma^{-1}\frac{1}{K_{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{n}}\left\|X_{i,k}-Y_{i}\right\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta-2}\left(X_{i,k}-Y_{i}\right), with X i , k X_{i,k} being samples from the correctly specified posterior P ( ⋅ ∣ Y = Y i ) P(\cdot\mid Y=Y_{i}) , and K n K_{n} denoting the Monte
Carlo budget such that the posterior-sampling error vanishes asymptotically.
For this, we assume a similar empirical L 2 L_{2} -consistency in the evaluation sample as we did for the other score
estimator: 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n sup θ ∈ Θ λ 2 ​ ‖ G ^ K n , β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) − G β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) ‖ 2 2 = o p ​ ( 1 ) . \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\lambda^{2}\left\|\widehat{G}_{K_{n},\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})-G_{\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}=o_{p}(1). Thus, the argument follows along similar lines to the proof above.
Defining a i ​ ( θ ) = Δ s ^ m , n ​ ( Y i ) − λ ​ G β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) , Δ i ​ ( θ ) = Δ λ ⁡ ( G ^ K n , β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) − G β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) ) . a_{i}(\theta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\widehat{s}_{m,n}(Y_{i})-\lambda G_{\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i}),\quad\Delta_{i}(\theta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{=}}\lambda\left(\widehat{G}_{K_{n},\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})-G_{\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})\right). Then the Monte Carlo residual satisfies s ^ m , n ​ ( Y i ) − λ ​ G ^ K n , β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) = a i ​ ( θ ) − Δ i ​ ( θ ) . \widehat{s}_{m,n}(Y_{i})-\lambda\widehat{G}_{K_{n},\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})=a_{i}(\theta)-\Delta_{i}(\theta). Therefore, for each θ ∈ Θ \theta\in\Theta , J ^ n , K n MC ​ ( θ ) − J ^ n ​ ( θ ) \displaystyle\widehat{J}_{n,K_{n}}^{\mathrm{MC}}(\theta)-\widehat{J}_{n}(\theta) = 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n ( ‖ a i ​ ( θ ) − Δ i ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 − ‖ a i ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 ) \displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\|a_{i}(\theta)-\Delta_{i}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}-\|a_{i}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}\right) = 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n ( − 2 ​ ⟨ a i ​ ( θ ) , Δ i ​ ( θ ) ⟩ + ‖ Δ i ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 ) . \displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(-2\langle a_{i}(\theta),\Delta_{i}(\theta)\rangle+\|\Delta_{i}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}\right). Taking the supremum and applying Cauchy–Schwarz as before gives sup θ ∈ Θ | J ^ n , K n MC ​ ( θ ) − J ^ n ​ ( θ ) | \displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\left|\widehat{J}_{n,K_{n}}^{\mathrm{MC}}(\theta)-\widehat{J}_{n}(\theta)\right| ≤ 2 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n sup θ ∈ Θ ‖ a i ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 ​ ‖ Δ i ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 + 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n sup θ ∈ Θ ‖ Δ i ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 \displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|a_{i}(\theta)\|_{2}\|\Delta_{i}(\theta)\|_{2}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|\Delta_{i}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2} ≤ 2 ​ ( 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n sup θ ∈ Θ ‖ a i ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 ) 1 / 2 ​ ( 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n sup θ ∈ Θ ‖ Δ i ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 ) 1 / 2 \displaystyle\leq 2\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|a_{i}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|\Delta_{i}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}\right)^{1/2} + 1 n ∑ i = 1 n sup θ ∈ Θ ∥ Δ i ( θ ) ∥ 2 2 . \displaystyle+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|\Delta_{i}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}. The second part of the first term and the last term are o p ​ ( 1 ) o_{p}(1) by assumption, since: ‖ Δ i ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 = λ 2 ​ ‖ G ^ K n , β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) − G β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) ‖ 2 2 . \|\Delta_{i}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}=\lambda^{2}\left\|\widehat{G}_{K_{n},\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})-G_{\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}. Thus, the question is what is the order of 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n sup θ ∈ Θ ‖ a i ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|a_{i}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2} ?
Reusing notation from the first part of the proof: e i = s ^ m , n ​ ( Y i ) − s m ​ ( Y i ) , r i ​ ( θ ) = s m ​ ( Y i ) − λ ​ G β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) , e_{i}=\widehat{s}_{m,n}(Y_{i})-s_{m}(Y_{i}),\quad r_{i}(\theta)=s_{m}(Y_{i})-\lambda G_{\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i}), we have: a i ​ ( θ ) = s ^ m , n ​ ( Y i ) − λ ​ G β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) = e i + r i ​ ( θ ) . a_{i}(\theta)=\widehat{s}_{m,n}(Y_{i})-\lambda G_{\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})=e_{i}+r_{i}(\theta). Now, using the triangle inequality: 1 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n sup θ ∈ Θ ‖ a i ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 ≤ 2 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n ‖ e i ‖ 2 2 + 2 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n sup θ ∈ Θ ‖ r i ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 . \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|a_{i}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|e_{i}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|r_{i}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}. The first term is, as discussed, o p ​ ( 1 ) o_{p}(1) by assumption; for the second term we have: 2 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n sup θ ∈ Θ ‖ r i ​ ( θ ) ‖ 2 2 = 2 n ​ ∑ i = 1 n sup θ ∈ Θ ‖ s m ​ ( Y i ) − λ ​ G β , Σ ​ ( Y i ) ‖ 2 2 = O p ​ ( 1 ) \frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|r_{i}(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\left\|s_{m}(Y_{i})-\lambda G_{\beta,\Sigma}(Y_{i})\right\|_{2}^{2}=O_{p}(1) by assumption on the oracle residual of the Energy-Tweedie term in the Proposition.
Thus: sup θ ∈ Θ | J ^ n , K n MC ​ ( θ ) − J ^ n ​ ( θ ) | = o p ​ ( 1 ) . \sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\left|\widehat{J}_{n,K_{n}}^{\mathrm{MC}}(\theta)-\widehat{J}_{n}(\theta)\right|=o_{p}(1). By the triangle inequality, we now have: sup θ ∈ Θ | J ^ n , K n MC ​ ( θ ) − J 0 ​ ( θ ) | ≤ sup θ ∈ Θ | J ^ n , K n MC ​ ( θ ) − J ^ n ​ ( θ ) | + sup θ ∈ Θ | J ^ n ​ ( θ ) − J 0 ​ ( θ ) | = o p ​ ( 1 ) . \sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\left|\widehat{J}_{n,K_{n}}^{\mathrm{MC}}(\theta)-J_{0}(\theta)\right|\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\left|\widehat{J}_{n,K_{n}}^{\mathrm{MC}}(\theta)-\widehat{J}_{n}(\theta)\right|+\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}|\widehat{J}_{n}(\theta)-J_{0}(\theta)|=o_{p}(1). Thus, for any θ ^ n , K n MC \widehat{\theta}^{\mathrm{MC}}_{n,K_{n}} satisfying: J ^ n , K n MC ​ ( θ ^ n , K n MC ) ≤ inf θ ∈ Θ J ^ n , K n MC ​ ( θ ) + o p ​ ( 1 ) \widehat{J}_{n,K_{n}}^{\mathrm{MC}}(\widehat{\theta}^{\mathrm{MC}}_{n,K_{n}})\leq\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\widehat{J}_{n,K_{n}}^{\mathrm{MC}}(\theta)+o_{p}(1) the same argument as above applies, giving us: θ ^ n , K n MC → P θ ∗ . \widehat{\theta}^{\mathrm{MC}}_{n,K_{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}\theta^{*}. ∎ Proof of Corollary A.3 Starting from the population objective J 0 ​ ( β , λ , Σ ) J_{0}(\beta,\lambda,\Sigma) , and fixing β \beta and Σ \Sigma , define G ( y ; β , Σ ) = Δ Σ − 1 𝔼 X ∼ P ( ⋅ ∣ Y = y ) [ ∥ X − y ∥ Σ − 1 β − 2 ( X − y ) ] . noise law is the generalized Gaussian of Eq. 16 with the trace-normalized shape matrix S ⁡ ( u , v ) = ( 1 + u v v 1 − u ) S(u,v)=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}1+u&v\\

Eq. 42 with d = 2 d=2 . The schedules below prescribe r t r_{t} , which fixes the reference RMS ρ t = r t ​ λ t 1 / β t \rho_{t}=r_{t}\,\lambda_{t}^{1/\beta_{t}} along any path.

Fixed covariance paths are shown as points; the dashed circle bounds the training region. The Gaussian/isotropic path is ordinary Gaussian diffusion, where Eq. 32 reduces to
the posterior-mean Tweedie residual. The joint path starts at the Gaussian/isotropic start and ends at the
generalized/non-diagonal endpoint, so the corruption transitions continuously from isotropic Gaussian into a heavy-tailed anisotropic law as the noise shrinks, and was not tuned for performance. Sampling uses annealed Langevin with 1,000 1{,}000 updates at the first level and 100 100 at each subsequent level, N = 256 N=256 posterior draws per score evaluation, a shared step-size curve, and a final posterior draw at the
terminal level r t = 0.197 r_{t}=0.197 . Each path is sampled with three seeds ( 2718 2718 , 3141 3141 , 5772 5772 ) of 256 256 images,
pooled to 768 768 images per path; every generated image enters the evaluation without any selection. Evaluation. The primary metric is the signed unbiased energy distance between penultimate-layer
features of a fixed, publicly available MNIST classifier (ONNX Model Zoo MNIST-12 ; 256 256 -dimensional
features; test accuracy 0.989 0.989 ), computed between generated samples and a fixed bank of 2,048 2{,}048 held-out real
images. The classifier features discard pixel-level nuisance variation while any discrepancy in feature space
shows a discrepancy between the underlying distributions; the energy distance admits an unbiased estimator and
equals twice the squared maximum mean discrepancy for the associated distance-induced kernel [ 42 ; 32 ] , making it a close relative of the kernel inception distance [ 3 ] while remaining within the energy-score toolkit used
throughout this paper. Because the estimator is unbiased, its scale has a direct null reference: repeated
real-vs-real draws of disjoint held-out images with the identical estimator and reference bank give a null
distribution whose 95 95 th percentile at the pooled sample size is 0.017 0.017 . Companion metrics are the mean
classifier confidence and the total variation between the predicted class distribution and the test-set class
prior. Reported values are pooled point estimates over 768 768 images per path with seed-stratified 95 % 95\% bootstrap confidence intervals. The real-vs-real feature energy distance is reported without a percentile
interval because the energy distance is a degenerate U-statistic at the null; the measured null distribution
supplies its reference scale instead.
