A.7 Further Connections We present some further connective threads to the work presented in this section, specializing again to the generalized Gaussian case. A.7.1 Connection Between the Noise Geometry and the Energy Score Loss As indicated by Theorem 20 , there is a correspondence between each noising distribution
parameterized by β , λ , Σ \beta,\lambda,\Sigma and an energy score with the same parameters whose
path-derivative matches the score of the data noised by the former. One perspective is to notice that the Mahalanobis distance ∥ ⋅ ∥ Σ − 1 \|\cdot\|_{\Sigma^{-1}} can be associated to the
(position-independent) Riemannian inner product ⟨ u , v ⟩ = u T ​ Σ − 1 ​ v \langle u,v\rangle=u^{T}\Sigma^{-1}v . We thus get for the
Riemannian gradient grad Σ − 1 ⁡ f ⁡ ( x ) = Σ ​ ∇ x f ​ ( x ) . \operatorname{grad}_{\Sigma^{-1}}f(x)=\Sigma~\nabla_{x}f(x). Now, one can define the
Riemannian path-derivative analogously to the ordinary one, and get that, by Eq. 17 the
noisy score is proportional to the Riemannian gradient of some potential/energy: s m ​ ( y ) = − λ ​ Σ − 1 ​ 𝔼 ​ [ ‖ y − X ‖ Σ − 1 β − 2 ​ ( y − X ) ∣ Y = y ] = − λ ​ Σ − 1 ​ grad Σ − 1 PD ⁡ φ ⁡ ( y ) , s_{m}(y)=-\lambda~\Sigma^{-1}\,{\mathbb{E}}\left[\|y-X\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta-2}~(y-X)\mid Y=y\right]=-\lambda~\Sigma^{-1}\operatorname{grad}^{\mathrm{PD}}_{\Sigma^{-1}}\varphi(y), where we can solve for φ ⁡ ( y ) \varphi(y) and obtain: φ ⁡ ( y ) = 1 β ​ 𝔼 ​ [ ‖ y − X ‖ Σ − 1 β ∣ Y = y ] , \varphi(y)=\frac{1}{\beta}~{\mathbb{E}}[\|y-X\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{\beta}\mid Y=y], i.e., the “mean β \beta -energy” associated with the posterior (w.r.t. Y Y ) of the generalized Gaussian
elliptical distribution (i.e., “energy model”) — cf. Eq. 16 — in the geometry induced by Σ \Sigma . A.7.2 Denoising Denoising can be seen as both a problem setting and a generative modeling approach [ 23 ] . In this subsection, we first focus on the denoising problem as such, with the
generative modeling presented in Sec. 5.3 . In this case, the posterior P ⁡ ( X ∣ Y = y ) P(X\mid Y=y) can be considered a distributional denoiser with P θ ​ ( X ∣ Y = y ) P_{\theta}(X\mid Y=y) denoting its estimator. An example of score-based denoising is Kim and Ye [17] , who have focused
on using the standard Tweedie formula in the denoising context, using denoising autoencoders [ 46 ] to extract the score. The vast majority of denoising work limits the noise to the
isotropic Gaussian 𝒩 ⁡ ( 0 , σ 2 ​ I ) {\mathcal{N}}(0,\sigma^{2}I) . An interesting perspective is to consider ideal denoisers in light of our results.
As noted in Milanfar and Delbracio [23] , if f ⁡ ( y , α ) f(y,\alpha) denotes the denoising functional for noise level
parameters α \alpha 4 4 4 One can consider α \alpha to be a vector in this case. , then the ideal denoiser will be a gradient of some scalar function (the “energy” or potential): f ⋆ ​ ( y , α ) = ∇ 𝒰 ​ ( y , α ) , f^{\star}(y,\alpha)=\nabla{\mathcal{U}}(y,\alpha), which is equivalent to requiring that its Jacobian be symmetric in the isotropic Gaussian case. Thus, the
denoiser itself defines a conservative vector field . We will be focusing on MSE-optimal denoisers in the
following discussion. In the isotropic Gaussian case, this is readily apparent from the “basic” Tweedie formula: f ⋆ ​ ( y , σ 2 ) = 𝔼 ⁡ [ X ∣ Y = y ] = y + σ 2 ​ ∇ y ​ log ⁡ m ⁡ ( y ) f^{\star}(y,\sigma^{2})\;=\;{\mathbb{E}}[X\!\mid Y{=}y]\;=\;y+\sigma^{2}\,\nabla_{y}\log m(y) implies for the Jacobian: ∇ f ⋆ ​ ( y , σ 2 ) = I + σ 2 ​ ∇ 2 ​ log ⁡ m ⁡ ( y ) , \nabla f^{\star}(y,\sigma^{2})=I+\sigma^{2}\nabla^{2}\!\log m(y), which is obviously symmetric. Moving to the anisotropic Gaussian case — α = ( β = 2 , λ = 1 , Σ ) \alpha=(\beta=2,\lambda=1,\Sigma) — we still have
Eq. 5 : f ⋆ ​ ( y , α ) = y + Σ ​ ∇ y ​ log ⁡ m ⁡ ( y ) . f^{\star}(y,\alpha)\;=\;y+\Sigma\,\nabla_{y}\log m(y). By App. A.7.1 , this can be seen as the Riemannian gradient in the geometry induced by Σ \Sigma : f ⋆ ​ ( y , α ) = grad Σ − 1 ⁡ ( 1 2 ​ ‖ y ‖ Σ − 1 2 + log ⁡ m ⁡ ( y ) ) ⏟ 𝒰 , f^{\star}(y,\alpha)\;=\;\operatorname{grad}_{\Sigma^{-1}}\!\underbrace{\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}\|y\|^{2}_{\Sigma^{-1}}+\log m(y)\Big)}_{{\mathcal{U}}}, (54) where 𝒰 {\mathcal{U}} is the potential function mentioned above for this case.
The Jacobian of such an ideal denoiser: ∇ f ⋆ ​ ( y ) = I + Σ ​ ∇ 2 ​ log ⁡ m ⁡ ( y ) \nabla f^{\star}(y)=I+\Sigma\nabla^{2}\!\log m(y) is not necessarily symmetric (as it depends on Σ \Sigma , i.e. the anisotropy). However, we have: ( ∇ f ⋆ ( y ) ) ⊤ Σ − 1 = Σ − 1 ∇ f ⋆ ( y ) , (\nabla f^{\star}(y))^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}=\Sigma^{-1}\nabla f^{\star}(y), (55) i.e., it is self-adjoint in the Σ − 1 \Sigma^{-1} geometry, or equivalently, it is symmetric in whitened coordinates.
This represents a generalization of the criterion for ideal denoisers to anisotropic Gaussian noise — self-adjointness . A common alternative MAP-optimal denoiser f MAP ​ ( y , α ) f_{\mathrm{MAP}}(y,\alpha) can be obtained from the proximal operator [ 23 ; 4 ] : prox R Σ − 1 ⁡ ( y ) = arg ​ min x ⁡ ( 1 2 ​ ‖ x − y ‖ Σ − 1 2 + R ⁡ ( x ) ) , \operatorname{prox}_{R}^{\Sigma^{-1}}(y)=\argmin_{x}(\tfrac{1}{2}\|x-y\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2}+R(x)), where R ⁡ ( x ) R(x) is the negative log-prior on the clean data x x . Under additive Gaussian noise, the MAP estimator is the proximal
operator of the negative log-prior. If the prior is also Gaussian, then the posterior is Gaussian, so the MAP
estimate coincides with the posterior mean (equivalently, the Wiener filter); hence the resulting field matches
Eq. 54 . Finally, still in the anisotropic Gaussian case, we have by Tweedie: f ⋆ ​ ( y ) − y = Σ ​ s m ​ ( y ) . f^{\star}(y)-y=\Sigma\,s_{m}(y)\;. However, by Thm. 20 , we also have: f ⋆ ​ ( y ) − y = − Σ 2 ​ ∇ y PD ES Σ − 1 , 2 ​ ( P ⁡ ( X ∣ Y = y ) , y ) . f^{\star}(y)-y=-\frac{\Sigma}{2}\nabla_{y}^{\mathrm{PD}}\mathrm{ES}_{\Sigma^{-1},2}\big(P(X\mid Y=y),y\big). (56) Thus, the potential 𝒰 {\mathcal{U}} that generates the conservative field is defined either by the log-marginal log ⁡ m ⁡ ( y ) \log m(y) (as previously shown in Eq. 54 ) or the
corresponding energy score as denoted by Eq. 56 : grad Σ − 1 ⁡ ( 1 2 ​ ‖ y ‖ Σ − 1 2 + log ⁡ m ⁡ ( y ) ) \displaystyle\operatorname{grad}_{\Sigma^{-1}}\!\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}\|y\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2}\,+\,\log m(y)\Big) = f ⋆ ​ ( y ) \displaystyle=\;f^{\star}(y) (57) = grad Σ − 1 PD ⁡ ( 1 2 ​ ‖ y ‖ Σ − 1 2 − 1 2 ​ ES Σ − 1 , 2 ​ ( P ⁡ ( X ∣ Y = y ) , y ) ) , \displaystyle=\;\operatorname{grad}^{\mathrm{PD}}_{\Sigma^{-1}}\!\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}\|y\|_{\Sigma^{-1}}^{2}\;-\;\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{ES}_{\Sigma^{-1},2}\!\big(P(X\mid Y=y),y\big)\Big), with the caveat that in the latter case, we need to take the path derivative ∇ y PD \nabla_{y}^{\mathrm{PD}} (Eq. 14 ). A.8 Model Architectures and Training Our posterior models adapt the implementation of Shen and Meinshausen [35] 5 5 5 https://github.com/xwshen51/engression : stochastic residual MLPs conditioned on the noise parameters,
with architectures summarized in Table 14 .
We train these models on clean–noisy pairs using the Euclidean energy score with exponent one for the non-diffusion use cases.
Noise parameters enter through feature-wise linear modulation (FiLM): the noise scale for isotropic
models, scale and covariance shape for parameter estimation, and all five noise parameters for the
five-parameter Eight-Gaussians and MNIST models. Posterior ( L , H , Z ) (L,H,Z) FiLM width Batch size (k) Learning rate Steps (k) Gaussian, isotropic ( 6,200,100 ) (6,200,100) 16 16 40 40 2 ⋅ 10 − 5 → 2 ⋅ 10 − 6 2\cdot 10^{-5}\to 2\cdot 10^{-6} 240 240 Generalized, isotropic ( 6,100,100 ) (6,100,100) linear 80 80 3 ⋅ 10 − 5 → 2 ⋅ 10 − 6 3\cdot 10^{-5}\to 2\cdot 10^{-6} 200 200 Five-parameter ( 6,250,100 ) (6,250,100) 64 64 40 40 1.5 ⋅ 10 − 5 → 10 − 6 1.5\cdot 10^{-5}\to 10^{-6} 280 280 Parameter estimation ( 6,250,100 ) (6,250,100) 64 64 40 40 1.5 ⋅ 10 − 5 → 10 − 6 1.5\cdot 10^{-5}\to 10^{-6} 280 280 MNIST ( 16 , 2560 , 128 ) (16,2560,128) 256 256 1.536 1.536 1.5 ⋅ 10 − 5 → 1.5 ⋅ 10 − 6 1.5\cdot 10^{-5}\to 1.5\cdot 10^{-6} 100 100 Table 14: Posterior architectures and training budgets. The first four rows use Eight Gaussians. L L counts affine layers in the residual branches (two per block), H H is the hidden width, and Z Z is the Gaussian noise dimension per layer. FiLM width is the hidden width of the conditioning MLP;
“linear” denotes a single affine map. Batch sizes and optimizer steps are reported in thousands. All posteriors use Adam with cosine learning-rate decay. Eight-Gaussians training accumulates two
minibatches per optimizer step; MNIST uses one, with a 500 500 -step learning-rate warmup.
Each fixed or five-parameter Eight-Gaussians posterior is reused for score estimation and diffusion;
all five MNIST sampling paths share one posterior model. The five-parameter Eight-Gaussians posterior draws one noise-parameter combination per minibatch: with probability 0.35 0.35 one of 18 18 preselected combinations, namely ( β , λ ) = ( 1.4 , 1.8 ) (\beta,\lambda)=(1.4,1.8) at the reference RMS levels ρ ∈ { 0.32 , 0.57 , 0.95 } \rho\in\{0.32,0.57,0.95\} (Eq. 42 ) and the covariance shapes ( u , v ) ∈ { ( 0 , 0 ) , ( 0.6 , 0 ) , ( 0 , 0.6 ) , ( 0.6 / 2 , 0.6 / 2 ) } (u,v)\in\{(0,0),(0.6,0),(0,0.6),(0.6/\sqrt{2},0.6/\sqrt{2})\} together with six extremal parameter combinations, and
otherwise β \beta uniform on [ 1.3 , 2 ] [1.3,2] , λ \lambda and ρ \rho log-uniform on [ 1 , 2.5 ] [1,2.5] and [ 0.01 , 10 ] [0.01,10] , and ( u , v ) (u,v) uniform in area on the disk of radius 0.7 0.7 . For plug-in parameter estimation, we reuse one score estimation network across the three noise-level settings in each replicate. Conditioning on the known log noise level avoids training three separate Hyvärinen networks; the method itself is parameter-agnostic (i.e., this is to save on compute, not required by the method).
Each network has three hidden layers of width 256 256 with SiLU activations and is trained by exact Hyvärinen score matching, using AdamW for 40,000 40{,}000 steps with batch size 4,096 4{,}096 and learning rate 10 − 3 10^{-3} . Complete training configurations, seeds, and preprocessing are provided in the supplement. For the direct-score comparison in Sec. 5.1 , ET-DSM and Hyvärinen use the
MLP architecture described above, with seven inputs:
the two noisy coordinates and the normalized conditioning vector ( β , log ⁡ λ , log ⁡ ρ , u , v ) (\beta,\log\lambda,\log\rho,u,v) ,
with the reference RMS ρ \rho defined in Eq. 42 .
The linear output layer outputs the two score coordinates. We train each network for 80,000 80{,}000 AdamW updates with batch size 4,096 4{,}096 , cosine learning-rate
decay from 10 − 3 10^{-3} to 10 − 5 10^{-5} , zero weight decay, and gradient clipping at norm 10 10 .
Each minibatch uses one noise-parameter combination; the two estimators receive the same minibatches in order. NeurIPS Paper Checklist 1) Claims Question: Do the main claims made in the abstract and introduction accurately reflect the paper’s
contributions and scope? Answer: [Yes] Justification: The abstract and introduction state the paper’s main contributions: the Energy–Tweedie identity, its relationship to the classical setting, and its consequences for score estimation, noise-parameter estimation, and diffusion-style sampling. 2) Limitations Question: Does the paper discuss the limitations of the work performed by the authors? Answer: [Yes] Justification: The Discussion lists the limitations: the identity holds for the (large) additive Gibbs noise model, the noise-parameter estimation theory assumes a correctly specified posterior term and a sufficiently accurate auxiliary score estimator, the experiments are specialized to the generalized Gaussian case on low-dimensional synthetic data and MNIST, and large-scale image-generation benchmarks are out of scope. 3) Theory assumptions and proofs Question: For each theoretical result, does the paper provide the full set of assumptions and a complete
(and correct) proof? Answer: [Yes] Justification: The theoretical results state their assumptions in the main text or immediately adjacent discussion, and the formal proofs are provided in the appendix. 4) Experimental result reproducibility Question: Does the paper fully disclose all the information needed to reproduce the main experimental
results of the paper to the extent that it affects the main claims and/or conclusions of the paper
(regardless of whether the code and data are provided or not)? Answer: [Yes] Justification: The experiments use well-known synthetic datasets and MNIST, and all the settings and steps needed to
reproduce them are included. 5) Open access to data and code Question: Does the paper provide open access to the data and code, with sufficient instructions to
faithfully reproduce the main experimental results, as described in supplemental material? Answer: [Yes] Justification: The data used in the experiments are synthetically generated or public (MNIST); the code, configurations, trained models, and instructions for reproducing the experimental results are released at https://github.com/andleb/et . 6) Experimental setting/details Question: Does the paper specify all the training and test details (e.g., data splits, hyperparameters,
how they were chosen, type of optimizer) necessary to understand the results? Answer: [Yes] Justification: We specify the main details in the paper and the rest in the appendix and the public code repository. 7) Experiment statistical significance Question: Does the paper report error bars suitably and correctly defined or other appropriate
information about the statistical significance of the experiments? Answer: [Yes] Justification: Where applicable, the reported experimental results include error bars or equivalent uncertainty summaries for the experiments supporting the empirical claims. 8) Experiments compute resources Question: For each experiment, does the paper provide sufficient information on the computer resources
